## [RegretBench Exposes Coding Agents' Backup Blind Spot, BackUpper Fixes It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28934) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces RegretBench, a benchmark of 205 executable tasks derived from real-world regret moments on X.com, GitHub, and Stack Overflow, and finds that six frontier coding agents complete 86.1% of tasks on average but back up state before risky modifications only 15.2% of the time. The authors also propose BackUpper, a training-free harness plug-in that checks every tool call and preserves affected state before modification, covering every regret-moment action and restoring states with 92.2% average similarity at $0.014 per task. This work highlights a critical safety gap: strong task-completion ability does not imply that agents preserve state before destructive changes, which can lead to irreversible data loss in real software environments. BackUpper shows that reversibility can be added cheaply and with far fewer tokens than self-backup, making it relevant to agent-harness designers and anyone deploying autonomous coding agents. BackUpper combines rule-based strategies for common operations with a lightweight subagent for complex cases, and its rule table can expand over time from subagent decisions; it uses 7x fewer tokens than a main-agent self-backup baseline. When users specify backup preferences, BackUpper narrows its scope and matches human annotations on whether an action is worth preserving with an 84.5% F1 score.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Coding agents are LLM-driven systems that autonomously execute multi-step tasks by calling tools that modify files, environments, and configurations. A harness is the surrounding software layer that manages tool calls, permissions, and recovery for such agents. RegretBench is built from documented cases where users wished they had preserved state, and BackUpper is distributed as an npm package that hooks into an agent's tool-call loop.

<a id="item-56"></a>
## [MPPI-Inspired World Model Framework Enables LLM Agents to Self-Improve at Test Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28945) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.28945) proposes a test-time framework that adapts Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) control to LLM agents, having them propose multiple candidate actions and imagine their outcomes with a world model before acting. A dual-branch experience memory lets the planner branch refine action selection while the world model branch improves next-observation prediction, and experiments on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld across three backbones show strong overall performance. This work bridges sampling-based model predictive control and LLM agent research, offering a way for agents to improve during deployment without additional training or real-environment trial and error. If validated, such experience-guided predictive control could make deployed agents more sample-efficient and safer in interactive tasks. Because language environments provide neither explicit dynamics nor cost functions, the framework evaluates imagined outcomes for task progress and risk and uses MPPI-style weights over the resulting costs to guide the final decision. The approach is evaluated on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld with three backbone models, and the authors state the code will be publicly released.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) control is a sampling-based model predictive control algorithm, first presented at ICRA 2016, that samples many candidate action sequences, rolls them out through a dynamics model, and weights them by their predicted cost; it is widely used in robotics for motion planning in uncertain environments. World models are learned predictors of environment dynamics that let agents simulate outcomes before acting, and prior work such as WALL-E has combined them with model-predictive control for LLM agents. This paper brings those ideas into a test-time self-improvement loop for language agents.

<a id="item-57"></a>
## [AgentPolicyForge Builds Executable Policy Benchmarks and Trained Guards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28989) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AgentPolicyForge introduces a framework that uses executable environments to construct policy benchmarks and iteratively train policy guards, producing PolicyGym, a dataset spanning ten policy families with 698 training scenarios and 254 held-out human-reviewed test scenarios. The final guard-based configuration achieves a 40-44% relative reduction in attack success rate and fewer benign rejections compared to policy prompting alone. This work addresses a real and underexplored problem: agent policies provided only in prompts fail to reliably prevent violations, and the framework offers a systematic way to enforce policies through executable environments and trained guards. It could impact how organizations deploy customized agents with tool, resource, and execution-condition policies, improving safety without over-blocking legitimate actions. Every violating case in PolicyGym uses a permitted tool, requiring checks of arguments, resources, and context rather than tool identity alone; the case construction process supports iterative guard training where an attacker generates requests using retrieved strategies and the guard is fine-tuned on balanced, environment-verified examples. Training improves enforcement even with a fixed archive, and updating the archive further reduces in-domain attack success compared with keeping it fixed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are increasingly deployed with customized policies that restrict which tools they can use, on which resources, and under what execution conditions. Simply including these policies in the agent's prompt does not reliably prevent violations, because agents may misuse permitted tools in ways that violate the policy's intent. A guard is a separate model or component trained to detect policy violations in agent tool calls, and training such guards requires examples of both compliant and violating calls, which are costly to obtain manually.

## [Multi-Agent AI Combines DRL and LLM for Proton Radiotherapy Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29010) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint presents an evidence-driven multi-agent compound AI system that integrates deep reinforcement learning (DRL) with a large language model (LLM) for head and neck proton pencil beam scanning (PBS) radiotherapy planning. The system uses a PPO-trained policy network to generate multiple candidate plans, then employs a Qwen3-14B-based planning agent, post-trained with SFT and GRPO, to refine plans through natural language conversation, and was evaluated on 21 patients with 5 plans each. This work demonstrates a novel integration of reinforcement learning and LLM-based multi-agent systems for a high-stakes clinical application, potentially enabling more personalized and efficient proton therapy planning. If validated, it could reduce manual planning burden and improve trade-off decisions in radiotherapy, impacting clinicians and patients in cancer care. The system comprises a planning agent that recommends evidence-supported adjustments, a chatbot for conversational interface, and an evaluation agent that retrieves clinical metrics and identifies constraint violations. The planning agent, based on Qwen3-14B, was post-trained with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and group relative policy optimization (GRPO) to enhance domain-specific reasoning, and showed improved instruction-following and information retrieval compared to the base LLM.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Proton pencil beam scanning (PBS) is an advanced proton therapy delivery mode that uses a narrow beam to scan tumors, allowing precise dose conformality. Treatment planning for PBS requires balancing competing objectives to meet clinical criteria while accommodating physician preferences, which is complex and time-consuming. Deep reinforcement learning, particularly Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), has been explored for automating treatment planning, while LLM-based multi-agent systems are emerging for clinical workflow support.

## [GenXR-Bench: First Benchmark for Coding Agents in Interactive XR Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29014) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced GenXR-Bench, the first benchmark for end-to-end evaluation of coding agents building interactive extended reality (XR) applications, comprising 112 expert-authored tasks (24 feature implementations, 21 bug repairs, and 67 application generation tasks). Its held-out test suite combines scripted and adaptive interactions with programmatic and visual assertions, achieving 96.5% accuracy against blinded expert judgments on 124 candidate applications run on consumer XR hardware. This benchmark fills a gap in coding-agent evaluation by moving beyond single-file or repository-scale software tasks into spatially situated, runtime-sensitive XR development, where robustness depends on user motion, spatial input, and environmental sensing. It provides a reproducible testbed that could shape how agent capabilities are measured in spatial computing and highlight domain-specific weaknesses before deployment. Evaluating 10 models, the strongest configuration achieved only 50.9% overall Pass@1, and failure analysis identified recurring misunderstandings in asynchronous sensor lifecycles, physical grounding, and embodied interaction semantics. The testing suite's 96.5% agreement with expert judgments was validated on 124 candidate applications executed on consumer XR hardware.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Coding agents are AI systems that can autonomously write, modify, and debug code across multi-step tasks, and they are increasingly evaluated on benchmarks that mimic real software engineering. Extended reality (XR) encompasses augmented, virtual, and mixed reality applications, which unlike traditional software must operate in dynamic 3D physical environments and respond to continuous user motion, spatial input, and sensor data. Existing benchmarks largely focus on conventional code generation or repository-level tasks, leaving interactive XR development—where runtime behavior and physical grounding are critical—largely unevaluated.

## [LLM+CPG Method Synthesizes ACSL Frame Specifications for C Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29046) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.29046) proposes an LLM-based approach that analyzes code property graphs (CPGs) to extract frame-relevant information and guide the synthesis of ACSL frame specifications from C code. On a benchmark of 84 ACSL-compliant functions from real-world systems, the method generates error-free frame specifications for 92.9% of functions, a 179% relative improvement over state-of-the-art techniques. Frame conditions are essential for formal verification because they specify which memory locations a function may modify, yet prior LLM-based specification synthesis has largely neglected them. This work fills that gap and, when integrated as an auxiliary component into existing LLM-based ACSL-synthesis frameworks, improves frame-specification generation performance by up to 61.9%, potentially benefiting formal verification and program analysis of C code. The approach captures both region-separation constraints and data dependencies that govern variable modifications, and on a second benchmark of 51 functions the success rate rises from 80.4% to 96.1%. The method relies on CPGs, a graph representation that unifies syntactic structure, control flow, and data dependencies, to supply the LLM with frame-relevant context.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: ACSL (ANSI/ISO C Specification Language) is a specification language for C, written as annotation comments and verified by tools such as Frama-C. A frame condition (e.g., an assigns clause) states which memory locations a function may write during execution; without one, verification tools default to assuming any memory may change, making proofs very difficult. Code property graphs (CPGs) are a program representation that merges abstract syntax trees, control flow graphs, and data flow information into a single queryable graph, widely used in program analysis.

## [LLMs Show Up to 90-Point Accuracy Swing Based on Instance Order](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29057) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.29057) evaluates fifteen LLMs across five datasets and seven instance order patterns on a multi-instance processing task where the correct answer is order-invariant. It finds that accuracy can differ by about 90 percentage points between the best and worst order patterns, with models generally performing better when majority-label instances appear at the start. This reveals a significant and previously understudied robustness vulnerability: LLM outputs for aggregated predictions can be systematically biased by input order, undermining reliability in applications like review analysis or social media monitoring. Practitioners should evaluate and mitigate order effects before deploying multi-instance LLM pipelines. The study holds each instance set fixed across order patterns, isolating order as the variable, and uses reasoning traces and gradient-based attribution for a subset of models. Traces disproportionately mention early instances, while attribution scores are generally higher near the beginning and end than in the middle, echoing position biases seen in other LLM contexts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-instance processing (MIP) requires an LLM to combine information from multiple independent inputs—such as product reviews or social media posts—into a single aggregated answer. In the studied task, the model must infer a binary label for each instance and identify the most frequent label, an operation that should be invariant to the order in which instances are presented. Prior work has documented order sensitivity in multimodal LLMs and multiple-choice questions, but MIP aggregation had received less attention.

## [Trace-level divergence reveals multilingual LLM reasoning gaps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29092) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.29092) introduces a trace-level divergence framework that decomposes agreement between multilingual reasoning traces into structural similarity, order consistency, and holistic similarity. Across three reasoning benchmarks, seven languages, and six frontier LLMs, it finds that 52.1%–67.3% of examples in the top quartile of holistic similarity are not in the top quartile of structural similarity. This work shows that standard answer-level and holistic similarity evaluations can substantially overestimate the consistency of multilingual reasoning traces, which matters for AI safety auditing, education, and verification in multilingual deployments. It motivates adopting trace-level evaluation to ensure users across languages receive comparable supporting evidence. The dissociation is stable across sentence encoders and step segmentations, and blind bilingual annotation studies in two English-anchored language pairs show human judgments align with structural similarity rather than the holistic baseline. A same-language control reveals that divergence is language-specific: two English traces for paraphrases of the same question agree more closely than an English trace and its counterparts in other languages.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Multilingual large language models increasingly produce visible reasoning traces (chain-of-thought) that support verification, education, and safety auditing. Standard evaluations typically measure only final-answer agreement or holistic response similarity, such as cosine similarity between whole-response embeddings, which establishes outcome consistency but not whether the supporting evidence is comparable across languages. This paper argues for evaluating the structure and order of reasoning steps, not just the final answer or overall semantic similarity.

## [GRAM: Group-wise Rank-Aware Model Merging via Subspace Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29110) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GRAM introduces a group-wise rank-aware model merging method that uses principal-angle geometry to group geometrically compatible tasks before performing SVD-based merging within each group. The approach provably reduces per-task projection error caused by subspace interference and outperforms prior methods on GLUE with Flan-T5, 8-task CLIP vision, and medical instruction tuning with Qwen2-7B-Instruct. This work provides a theoretical insight into subspace interference and a provable grouping strategy, which could improve multi-task model deployment, especially in privacy-sensitive domains like healthcare where models are fine-tuned locally and only weights can be shared. It also advances the broader field of model merging by addressing geometric misalignment among task subspaces. GRAM requires only the fine-tuned weights at merge time and uses just one additional model (K=2) to significantly mitigate subspace interference. The method is evaluated on language understanding (GLUE with Flan-T5), 8-task CLIP vision, and medical instruction tuning with four English/Chinese medical LoRAs on Qwen2-7B-Instruct.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Model merging combines multiple fine-tuned models into a single model without retraining, which is useful for multi-task deployment. However, when task updates occupy misaligned subspaces, projecting them onto a shared low-rank basis can discard task-relevant directions, a failure mode called subspace interference. Principal angles measure the alignment between subspaces, and GRAM uses this geometry to decide which tasks should be merged together.

## [StochIKM: Stochastic Reconstruction of Individual Patient Data from Kaplan-Meier Curves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29122) ⭐️ 7.0/10

StochIKM is a new stochastic algorithm that uses a vision-capable language model to digitize published Kaplan-Meier curves and returns a set of admissible individual patient datasets that exactly reproduce the risk table, follow the digitized curve within image resolution, and respect the printed median, interval, and event count. In simulations and 79 arms from published oncology trials, it reproduced published risk tables and medians more closely than LLM-improved IPDfromKM. Individual patient data are the gold standard for secondary analyses such as meta-analysis, but are rarely available; StochIKM's explicit fidelity constraints and admissible-set output provide a more rigorous and transparent way to reconstruct such data from published figures, which could improve evidence synthesis in health data science. The method guarantees a solution exists whenever the figure is read correctly, so failure indicates either a problem with the published figure or its digitization; however, the approach is niche, not yet peer-reviewed, and has not been validated at scale.

Individual patient data (IPD) reconstruction aims to recover patient-level data from such curves, often using tools like IPDfromKM, but existing methods return a single dataset even though the mapping from figure to data is not one-to-one. Vision-capable language models can extract text and coordinates from images, enabling automated digitization of plots.

## [NormTune: Norm-Guided PEFT Boosts Reasoning Task Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29134) ⭐️ 7.0/10

NormTune identifies norm-related under-adaptation as the cause of PEFT's performance gap on reasoning tasks and proposes a simple norm-guided strategy that adaptively strengthens under-updated layers. Experiments show that integrating NormTune into rank-8 LoRA, Bone, and MoRA yields average accuracy improvements of 24.24%, 11.33%, and 16.21%, respectively, across AIME 2024, MATH 500, GPQA, and AIME 2025. This work offers a practical way to close the performance gap between parameter-efficient fine-tuning and full fine-tuning on reasoning-intensive tasks, potentially reducing GPU costs for adapting large language models. It could benefit researchers and practitioners who rely on PEFT methods like LoRA for training on complex reasoning benchmarks. NormTune-enhanced rank-256 LoRA, Bone, and MoRA achieve performance comparable to S1 full fine-tuning, with average gains of 2.17%, 0.93%, and 3.00% over this baseline. The approach is based on three key observations linking the performance gap to smaller weight-update norms in PEFT compared to full fine-tuning.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) adapts large pre-trained models by updating only a small fraction of parameters, reducing computational costs. LoRA is a popular PEFT method that freezes pre-trained weights and injects trainable low-rank matrices into each layer. However, PEFT often underperforms full fine-tuning on reasoning-intensive tasks, and the reasons are not fully understood.

Researchers propose the Pitfall Anticipation World Model (PAWM), which selectively intervenes when a potential pitfall arises and predicts impending failure to steer black-box LLM agents away from it. PAWM improves Claude Sonnet 4.5 performance by 24.3% on ScienceWorld and 7.7% on WebArena-Lite. This work addresses a key question for world models in agentic tasks: what to predict and when to invoke them, moving beyond costly step-by-step observation prediction. It offers a more efficient and effective way to provide environment-specific foresight to black-box LLM agents, potentially improving their reliability in complex tasks. PAWM is trained in two stages: supervised fine-tuning on synthetic data labeled with agent pitfalls, followed by GRPO co-training with a black-box LLM to optimize it in the target environment. The approach focuses on bounded multi-step future prediction rather than per-step observations.

World models can provide this by predicting environment responses, but prior approaches that predict next observations at each step are costly and often uninformative. PAWM instead anticipates pitfalls within a bounded future, intervening only when needed.

## [TeachBack: Bidirectional Preference Optimization for On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29167) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TeachBack introduces a bidirectional preference-learning framework for on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) that trains the Teacher to generate pedagogically suitable trajectories, not just accurate ones, by controlling the length of the reference-reasoning suffix injected into the prompt and varying prompt style. At each rollout refresh, the shared policy produces both a privileged Teacher trajectory and a problem-only Student trajectory, and a pairwise preference objective improves the Student while the reversed preference shapes future Teacher supervision. This work addresses a real limitation in OPSD: teacher accuracy does not guarantee pedagogical suitability, since reference-conditioned derivations may omit exploration and self-correction. It could improve how LLMs learn reasoning from privileged information and is relevant to self-distillation and preference-optimization research. TeachBack maintains comparable Teacher accuracy while encouraging the Teacher to explore and re-solve problems, reducing reliance on the reference solution. Experiments on mathematical and logical reasoning tasks show consistent Student performance gains over existing OPSD methods and related baselines, though the work is a single arXiv preprint without community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) is a learning algorithm where a single LLM acts as both teacher and student with different contexts: the Teacher sees privileged information such as a reference solution, while the Student must solve problems without it. Preference optimization is a technique for aligning model outputs with desired behaviors using pairwise comparisons. TeachBack combines these ideas to shape not only what the Teacher knows but how it teaches.

## [CrashGym: Benchmarking the Decision Value of LLM Agent Experimentation in Market Crashes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29245) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CrashGym introduces a deterministic, replayable limit-order-book simulator where an LLM agent can run intervention experiments in a laboratory realization before committing to either a deleveraging intervention or a liquidity backstop in a target market crash scenario. The agent's choice is scored by regret against the best feasible intervention, computed by directly simulating all alternatives on independently seeded target repeats without human or model-based judging. This benchmark provides a rigorous, reproducible way to measure whether experimentation actually improves LLM agents' crisis decisions, moving beyond subjective evaluation. It could influence how agentic systems are evaluated in high-stakes domains like finance, where decision quality under uncertainty is critical. In a prospective study on 24 newly sampled worlds, laboratory access reduced the mean regret of the Luna deployment from 8.878 to 3.785, a paired reduction of 5.092 utility units (95% world-bootstrap interval: 1.028–9.507), or 57.4% of the observe-only mean. The simulator explicitly implements amplification channels—persistent price impact, liquidity withdrawal, margin-driven liquidation, and an evolving strategy mix—that can be switched on in stages, and an exploratory study found lower mean regret with laboratory access for all four deployments evaluated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Limit-order-book (LOB) simulators model financial exchanges by matching buy and sell orders, and are used to study market dynamics and train trading agents. Regret, in decision theory, measures the loss from not choosing the best alternative in hindsight; here it quantifies the value of experimentation by comparing an agent's decision to the best feasible intervention. LLM agents are increasingly deployed to make decisions in complex environments, but benchmarks rarely assess whether their pre-commitment experiments lead to better outcomes.

## [ARCANA Benchmark Exposes LLM Gaps in Far Analogical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29271) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ARCANA, a new benchmark for far analogical reasoning that requires mapping real-world objects or physical phenomena to abstract grid-based transformations. A human study showed people solve these tasks reliably, while evaluations across large language models revealed a distinct performance gap and systematic failure modes. Far analogical reasoning is considered a core component of human intelligence, and ARCANA's focus on cross-domain structural similarity distinguishes it from prior benchmarks like ARC-AGI. The demonstrated human-LLM gap suggests that current models still lack a fundamental reasoning capability, which matters for evaluating progress toward more general AI. The benchmark differs from ARC-AGI by emphasizing far analogical mappings rather than innate human priors, and the authors propose ConceptSIR, a framework based on concept slippage, introspection, and reflection that enables standard LLMs to solve several tasks unsolved by direct prompting. All materials, code, and task instances are publicly available via the project website and GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC), created by François Chollet, is a benchmark of grid-based visual puzzles designed to test abstraction and generalization, guided by the principle "easy for humans, hard for AI." Analogical reasoning involves identifying relational similarities between situations, and "far" analogies connect domains that are superficially dissimilar but share deep structural similarities. ARCANA builds on this tradition by testing whether models can map real-world concepts onto abstract grid transformations.

## [Gain-Loss Framing Shifts LLM Advice in 32.4% of High-Stakes Cases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29294) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces FrameBench-HPS, a controlled benchmark of 2,880 prompts derived from 240 high-stakes scenarios across healthcare, finance, education/career, and legal/consumer-rights domains. Testing seven LLMs, the authors find that expressing identical probabilities as gains versus losses changes the recommendation category in 32.4% of complete groups, with human evaluators confirming corresponding shifts in perceived risk and directiveness. This finding is practically important because LLMs are increasingly deployed to support consequential decisions, and advice that flips based on logically equivalent framing undermines trust and consistency. It highlights a safety-relevant failure mode that developers and users of decision-support systems need to test for and mitigate. Most framing-induced changes involve increased uncertainty rather than direct reversals, and the effects persist under conversational paraphrases and exceed ordinary repeated-generation variability. Probability canonicalization substantially reduces framing sensitivity, suggesting a practical intervention target.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: The framing effect is a well-documented cognitive bias in psychology where people respond differently to the same information depending on whether it is presented as a gain or a loss. LLMs are known to be sensitive to prompt formulation, but prior work had not systematically tested whether complementary gain and loss frames for identical probabilities alter high-stakes advice.

## [SpatialBrain: Learning Spatial Reasoning Experience with a Frozen VLM Backbone](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29317) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SpatialBrain is a new framework that learns reusable spatial reasoning experience—deciding when to observe, reconstruct, or measure—by separating scene evidence from reasoning experience, using a 3DFusionMemory and a Spatial Skill Library while keeping its vision-language backbone fixed. It improves over direct answers from the same backbone on four of five spatial-reasoning benchmarks and ties on the fifth. This work shows that spatial reasoning agents can improve through accumulated experience without retraining the underlying vision-language model, pointing toward more sample-efficient and adaptable embodied AI systems. It also introduces a clean separation between memory of the scene and memory of how to reason, which could inform future agent architectures. The framework consolidates completed trajectories into abstract process records between task blocks, so later decisions can draw on earlier execution without transferring scene coordinates or reference answers. The abstract is truncated, and evaluation is limited to five benchmarks with component comparisons and experience-coverage curves.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Spatial reasoning for AI agents involves deciding when to use geometric tools, when to observe directly, and when to change reference frames. Vision-language models (VLMs) provide a general backbone but often lack specialized spatial skills. Recent work explores agent memory and skill libraries to let models reuse past experience without retraining, and SpatialBrain builds on this trend by keeping the VLM frozen and learning only the reasoning process.

<a id="item-72"></a>
## [LLM agents can approximate Bayesian posteriors with reasoning and code tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29331) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.29331) presents a principled evaluation framework testing whether LLM agents can perform Bayesian belief updating in context, spanning qualitative epistemological examples, quantitative benchmarks, and analyses of non-Bayesian updates on downstream tasks. The study finds that LLMs generally violate Bayes' rule when updating beliefs in context, but their performance drastically improves when equipped with modern reasoning abilities and code execution, enabling close approximation of analytically intractable posteriors. This work challenges the assumption that LLMs cannot perform principled Bayesian inference and shows that current models with appropriate tools could already support complex belief updates in scientific discovery, potentially accelerating AI-powered research. It has implications for how LLMs are integrated into hypothesis generation, experimental design, and other evidence-accumulating tasks. The evaluation framework includes qualitative epistemological examples to probe probabilistic coherence, quantitative benchmarks of Bayesian inference capabilities, and analysis of how non-Bayesian updates affect downstream task performance. The key caveat is that without reasoning and code execution, LLMs generally fail to follow Bayes' rule, and even with tools they only closely approximate intractable posteriors rather than computing them exactly.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Bayesian inference is a method of updating beliefs about unknown quantities by combining a prior probability with a likelihood function derived from observed data, yielding a posterior probability according to Bayes' theorem. LLM agents are language models that can interact with tools and environments, often using in-context learning to adapt from information provided in the prompt. Prior work has suggested that such agents act as asymmetric or heuristic belief updaters, but this paper systematically tests whether they can instead perform principled Bayesian updating.

## [Event Transitions, Not Pixels, Drive World Model Return Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29435) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces a transition-level diagnostic that replaces imagined transitions with real ones to measure return error reduction in world models. It finds that restoring event transitions (steps where reward is earned or episode ends) removes a median of 70% of return error, while restoring transitions with the highest pixel, SSIM, or LPIPS error removes less than a random choice. This finding challenges the common practice of evaluating world models primarily by rendering quality, showing that visual fidelity does not guarantee accurate planning returns. It could shift how researchers design and repair world models for model-based reinforcement learning, emphasizing event-level accuracy over pixel-level metrics. Across 44 Atari-100k model-collection pairs, event patches outperform random patches in 39 cases (sign test p=1.4e-7) with a median gain of +44 percentage points. The remaining error is structured, and a rule recorded before four repair outcomes were known predicts when a diffusion model repairs these failures.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: World models are learned simulators that predict future states and rewards, allowing reinforcement learning agents to plan by imagining rollouts instead of interacting with the real environment. They are typically evaluated by how well they render frames, using metrics like pixel error, SSIM, and LPIPS, but a visually accurate rollout can still mispredict returns. The Atari-100k benchmark is a standard testbed for sample-efficient RL, where agents learn from only 100,000 interactions.

## [PreCoMem: Predictive Coding Memory for Long-Term Dialogue Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29467) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PreCoMem introduces a predictive-coding-inspired memory consolidation framework that represents user memory as confidence-weighted beliefs and uses an Effective Surprisal signal to gate updates into three deterministic actions: MAINTAIN, PROFILE, and CORRECT. Experiments on LoCoMo, LongMemEval-S, PersonaMem-v2, and the new DiMoBench show state-of-the-art accuracy in adapting to genuine belief drift while resisting transient noise. Long-horizon dialogue agents often struggle to balance stable personalization with adapting to genuine user changes, and this work offers a principled way to distinguish noise from real belief drift. It could improve memory systems for personalized assistants and other LLM applications that rely on long-term user modeling. The Effective Surprisal signal combines semantic deviation and contradiction evidence, and is downweighted when retrieval is diffuse and unreliable; a two-threshold gate then maps each turn to MAINTAIN, PROFILE, or CORRECT, with PROFILE acting as an explicit waiting room for ambiguous cues. The code is available on GitHub, and the new DiMoBench benchmark specifically tests non-stationary belief drift.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Predictive coding is a neuroscience-inspired theory that the brain constantly predicts incoming signals and updates internal models based on prediction errors, or surprisal. In dialogue agents, memory systems must decide when to store new information, when to reinforce existing beliefs, and when to overwrite outdated facts; naive approaches that append every observation or overwrite by heuristic rules often confuse transient noise with genuine user change.

## [RubricOpt Lets Policies Self-Revise Semantic Rubrics for DPO Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29491) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RubricOpt introduces a framework in which a policy diagnoses its own rubric-free outputs, retains requirements already covered by the rubric, and edits clauses that leave recurring failures unaddressed, with a deterministic gate checking evidence references and fixed semantic anchors. The revised rubric then guides Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) during training while preference pairs, labels, and the objective remain fixed, and is removed at evaluation. This work brings the behavioral specification itself into the policy's feedback loop, offering a new way to address recurring alignment failures beyond fixed preference labels. It is relevant to RLHF and preference-optimization research because it could make DPO training more targeted and interpretable, though gains and regressions vary across models and datasets. A matched continuation experiment isolates the contribution of rubric revision from additional training and finds mixed incremental effects for the tested edit, whose feedback overlaps the evaluation set. The analysis relates the local effect on rubric-free success to semantic alignment and a residual beyond the measured factors.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is a popular alignment method that trains a language model directly on pairwise preference data, avoiding a separate reward model and reinforcement learning loop. Rubrics are explicit, editable criteria used to specify desired model behavior, and recent work has explored using them as reward signals or judge guides. RubricOpt extends this line by letting the policy revise the rubric itself based on its own failures.

## [DPT: Penalizing Prefix Similarity to Diversify Parallel LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29501) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Diverse Parallel Thinking (DPT), a reinforcement learning method that penalizes prefix similarity among parallel reasoning paths to prevent mode collapse and encourage exploration. Experiments across multiple reasoning benchmarks show DPT outperforms prior parallel thinking methods and achieves state-of-the-art results, with code released on GitHub. Parallel thinking is a promising paradigm for boosting LLM reasoning, but RL training often collapses diverse paths into near-identical trajectories, undermining its core benefit. DPT directly addresses this failure mode, offering a simple technique that could make parallel reasoning more effective and broadly applicable in LLM training pipelines. DPT works by adding a penalty on the prefix similarity of parallel thinking paths to the RL reward, pushing the model toward distinct reasoning strategies rather than redundant ones. The authors validate path diversity as a key factor for improving parallel thinking and provide open-source code, though the abstract does not detail the penalty formulation or benchmark-specific gains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Parallel thinking refers to having a large language model explore multiple reasoning paths simultaneously, maintaining several hypotheses at once to avoid prematurely settling on a suboptimal solution. Reinforcement learning is commonly used to train such reasoning behaviors, but RL objectives can suffer from mode collapse, where the policy converges to a narrow set of similar outputs. Prefix similarity measures how much the beginnings of two reasoning traces overlap, so penalizing it discourages the model from repeating the same early steps across parallel paths.

## [TraceForge generates student-style reasoning traces for distillation without hidden teacher outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29680) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TraceForge proposes a method that takes a context and a teacher model's answer as input and generates a reasoning trace in the student model family's own style, enabling distillation without accessing restricted hidden reasoning from frontier models. Training is two-stage—supervised then adversarial—where a generator learns to regenerate the family's reasoning traces and a critic learns to judge authenticity; an independent judge scored generated traces at 0.68 versus 0.66 for real traces, and critic-selected candidates at 0.82. This addresses a growing practical problem: frontier providers now withhold detailed chain-of-thought from their APIs, blocking the common practice of distilling teacher reasoning into smaller models. A legal, ToS-compliant way to synthesize reasoning traces could let teams continue building strong student models without relying on extracted hidden outputs. On five benchmarks with everything else fixed, the generated reasoning matched other corpora on general tasks and beat Fable 5's own thinking content on coding, improving HumanEval+ by 3.7 points and MBPP+ by 7.1 at the final checkpoint with the same 5,000 questions and teacher answers. The authors note these are authenticity scores, not correctness rates, and the abstract is truncated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Knowledge distillation trains a smaller student model using outputs from a larger teacher model, and recent reasoning-focused LLMs often rely on chain-of-thought traces as training data. Leading providers now conceal step-by-step reasoning to protect intellectual property, and prior work showed encrypted reasoning blocks can be replayed across models to recover hidden traces—but that violates providers' terms of service. TraceForge instead synthesizes traces in the student's own style, avoiding the need for restricted teacher outputs.

## [Agent harness design matters for biomedical claim verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29812) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A controlled ablation on the SciFact dataset compares four agent harnesses for biomedical claim verification using the live PubMed API as the only tool. Letting a ReAct-style agent write its own PubMed queries triples gold-paper recall and achieves the best verdict accuracy (62%), while naive single-shot retrieval finds the gold paper only 6% of the time. This study provides rare controlled evidence that retrieval, not reasoning, is the main bottleneck in literature-searching agents, with gold abstracts yielding 79-86% accuracy versus 62% for the best realistic harness. It also shows that existence checks on cited PubMed IDs are insufficient safeguards, since closed-book models cite real but almost never relevant papers. The accuracy differences among realistic harnesses (49-62%) are not statistically significant at the study's sample size, and agents use less than half of their search budget. A citation audit found that closed-book models cited 327/335 PubMed IDs that exist, but an LLM judge rated only 3 of 327 as relevant, versus 67% relevance for citations from the search agent.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: SciFact is a benchmark of about 1.4K expert-written scientific claims paired with evidence-containing abstracts, used to evaluate claim verification systems. ReAct is a prompting framework that combines chain-of-thought reasoning with tool use, allowing an LLM to iteratively search and reason. PubMed's E-utilities API provides free programmatic access to over 35 million biomedical citations, making it a realistic retrieval tool for scientific agents.

## [UserSWE: A Multi-Turn Benchmark for Coding Agents with Dynamic Users](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29822) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced UserSWE, a new benchmark that evaluates coding agents on repository-level bug fixing through multi-turn conversations with simulated dynamic users, using execution-grounded verdicts at every turn. The benchmark was built from repository history and existing single-turn benchmarks, and eleven agents were evaluated on 72 tasks from 42 repositories. Existing coding benchmarks mostly present a complete issue in a single turn, which can produce false positives and false negatives and fails to capture the interactive nature of real developer-agent workflows. UserSWE shows that agent rankings change with the interaction budget, indicating that single-turn performance is insufficient to characterize interactive coding ability. At each turn, information pools constrain what the simulated user can reveal, while a persona chain sampled from a transition model calibrated on 20,859 development sessions governs how the user speaks. Tests run after every turn, and a pair of agents from different model families writes and gates each verdict, using test results as primary evidence and model judgment only for aspects tests cannot resolve.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Coding agents are LLM-based systems that can edit code, run tests, and interact with repositories to fix bugs. Benchmarks like SWE-bench evaluate them on single-turn issue reports, but real-world bug fixing often involves back-and-forth clarification with a user. UserSWE addresses this gap by simulating a dynamic user across multiple turns and grounding each verdict in test execution.

## [ToM2Vec learns continuous theory-of-mind tokens for multi-agent coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29934) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.29934) introduces ToM2Vec, which trains LLM agents to generate a fixed budget of continuous latent tokens that abstract a partner's mental state, supervised by the partner's actual thoughts. The method is evaluated on Overcooked-AI, multiparty negotiation, Decrypto, Hanabi, and Collab Escape/Capture, showing consistent gains over base models and trained or prompted baselines. Theory of mind is a core capability for effective collaboration, but LLM agents typically reason in text, which struggles to represent multiple uncertain partner states. ToM2Vec shows that latent reasoning is critical—performance drops when reasoning is expressed in text—and that the learned tokens generalize to unseen partner model families, including a proprietary model (GPT-5.4), suggesting a path toward more robust multi-agent coordination. The ToM tokens are generated at test time by each agent and act as conditioners for that agent's thoughts and actions, and analysis shows they improve the agent's ability to directly answer questions about partner beliefs, desires, and next actions, indicating they encode a theory of mind rather than task-specific information. The approach uses a fixed budget of continuous reasoning tokens, and the paper reports that latent reasoning outperforms text-based reasoning.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Theory of mind (ToM) is the ability to attribute mental states—beliefs, desires, intentions—to others, and in multi-agent systems it lets an agent anticipate how a partner's beliefs shape their behavior. Large language model (LLM) agents usually reason in natural language text, but text is a poor fit for representing multiple possible partner states under uncertainty. Latent reasoning, such as the Coconut approach, instead uses continuous hidden vectors as 'thoughts,' which can encode richer and more abstract representations than discrete word tokens. Benchmarks like Overcooked-AI test cooperative human-AI task performance, while Hanabi and Decrypto involve partial information and mixed motives.

## [System One decision models abstain better than LLMs on missing legal authority](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29961) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces System One decision models for legal authority verification, which return calibrated probabilities instead of generated text. In an extended CaseHOLD benchmark where the correct holding is replaced in 1,670 of 5,314 items, generative LLMs still pick an option 38–86% of the time when none is correct, while the most cautious decision model does so only 30% of the time. The work shows that legal AI should be evaluated by its wrong citations rather than accuracy alone, since generative LLMs frequently fabricate authority when the correct answer is absent. A decision model matches the best generative LLM on standard CaseHOLD (88.1% vs. 88.2%) at 1/55 the cost, making scalable legal verification more practical. Accepting an LLM's answer only when a decision model agrees halves its wrong citations, and distilling a decision model's probabilities into Qwen3-8B without labels yields a well-calibrated local model. The study compares four decision models with six generative LLMs on the extended CaseHOLD benchmark.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: System One decision models are built on large language models but, instead of generating text, return a calibrated probability for each predefined option, making them suitable for closed questions like legal authority verification. CaseHOLD is a legal benchmark dataset for multiple-choice holding identification, originally introduced by Zheng and Guha et al. in 2021. Abstention, or selective prediction, allows a model to decline answering when uncertain, which is critical in high-stakes domains such as law.

## [Behavioral Localization Distills Text Skills into Compact Soft Prompts for Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30041) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes Behavioral Localization, a method that identifies the small subset of trajectory positions where a text skill actually improves agent behavior, and uses only those positions to supervise a short soft prompt. On SpreadsheetBench with a frozen Qwen3.6-35B-A3B agent, the top 10% of positions captured 96.12% of the total positive skill gain, and selective supervision improved success from 30.36% (full trajectory) to 36.79% in the shared prompt setting and to 57.38% under task-specific conditions. This work shows that long text skills can be compressed into compact soft prompts without losing—and sometimes improving—performance, which could reduce inference costs and context length for LLM agents that rely on tool use. It also offers a principled way to avoid unnecessary behavioral changes when distilling skills from successful trajectories, relevant to agent research and deployment. The method compares the frozen agent with and without the text skill to find positions where the skill both increases the likelihood of successful tokens and meaningfully changes the next-token distribution. Results are limited to a single benchmark (SpreadsheetBench) and one model (Qwen3.6-35B-A3B), and the paper is an arXiv preprint without community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Soft prompts are learned continuous vectors prepended to a frozen language model's input, allowing efficient adaptation without full fine-tuning. Text skills are natural-language descriptions that guide agent behavior, but long descriptions increase computational cost. SpreadsheetBench is a challenging benchmark for spreadsheet manipulation derived from real-world scenarios, and Qwen3.6-35B-A3B is an open-weight mixture-of-experts model from the Qwen series.

## [HGM-Dual: Lineage-Aware, Diagnostic-Guided Optimization of Planning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30042) ⭐️ 7.0/10

HGM-Dual introduces a diagnostic-guided, lineage-aware framework for budget-efficient evolution of planning agents' prompts, tools, and workflows. It outperforms the strongest baseline by up to 14.45% relative improvement in case accuracy while matching HGM variants' final performance with 2x-4x fewer evaluation budget. This work addresses a known limitation of recursive self-improvement methods like HGM—budget inefficiency in planning tasks—by making the evolution process more targeted and interpretable. It could enable more practical and cost-effective optimization of LLM agents for complex, constraint-heavy planning domains. HGM-Dual represents agent evolution as lineage-aware context that records each ancestor's changes and outcomes, providing explicit edit-outcome attribution. It also introduces error-conditioned dual-path child generation, producing a general exploratory child and specialized children targeting the harness components most likely responsible for the parent's highest-ranked constraint violations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Harness optimization improves an agent's prompts, tools, and workflows without retraining the underlying LLM, which is especially useful for frozen or closed-source models. Leading approaches like the Darwin Gödel Machine and Huxley-Gödel Machine (HGM) use recursive self-improvement, where an agent modifies its own harness and validates changes on benchmarks, but these were mainly developed for coding tasks where the competence to improve aligns with the competence being improved. Planning tasks break this alignment because the agent must produce a sequence of tool calls satisfying interacting constraints, so open-ended self-modification often wastes evaluation budget on ineffective changes.

## [Charts as Epistemic Actions for Experience-Driven Data Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30072) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.30072) introduces a 200-task controlled benchmark, PROBE, showing that data agents can read self-generated charts as well as numeric output but often fail to use charts consistently in their reasoning loops. The authors propose a method that distills two families of experience from trajectory rollouts—how to select and verify analytical operations, and when to invoke text, charts, or both—raising analytical accuracy and visual evidence quality across three held-out benchmarks and four LLM backbones without changing model parameters. This work shifts the focus from whether agents can interpret charts to whether they choose to use them as internal reasoning steps, a gap that could limit the reliability of AI data-analysis agents on temporal, pattern, and anomaly tasks. If charts can be learned as epistemic actions without fine-tuning, it offers a lightweight path to more human-like, evidence-grounded agent reasoning. The benchmark uses four matched interface conditions and finds that charts lead on temporal, pattern, and anomaly reasoning while text leads on precise reading and conditional verification; the proposed method uses a trajectory judge to score outcomes and label intermediate steps, plus a validation gate that admits only experience improving held-out performance. Gains are reported across four LLM backbones, including backbones that never drew charts before.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Data agents built on ReAct-style loops alternate between reasoning and acting, but their intermediate reasoning is still almost entirely text, whereas human analysts move between numbers, tables, and charts. Epistemic actions are actions taken to gain information or reduce uncertainty during reasoning, and this paper asks whether plotting can serve as such an action inside an agent's loop. The work also relates to broader efforts to make AI reasoning more transparent and evidence-grounded.

## [First Passage Attention: Path Extrema Replace Summation in Forget-Gated Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22714) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces First Passage Attention (FPA), which replaces the monotonic path summation used in forget-gated attention with path extrema, lowering a key's logit based on the deepest dip of a learned scalar field below that key's level. FPA generalizes the Forgetting Transformer (FoX) as a special case and, in synthetic experiments, one-layer FPA heads converge to target key sets while softmax and FoX baselines fail on every seed. This work provides a novel theoretical approach to attention mechanisms with forget gates, enabling selective erasure of intermediate context while preserving older relevant tokens—a capability that monotonic decay cannot achieve. It could impact long-context sequence modeling and memory-efficient inference, as FPA uniquely achieves perfect passkey retrieval at 8× training length even with a compressed key-value cache. The paper shows that a constant-dimension FPA head can realize any subset of keys left alive by subsequent dips, whereas softmax heads with positional biases or token-level forget gates require a head dimension linear in the number of keys. In a 110M-parameter language model, FPA matches FoX in perplexity and achieves perfect passkey retrieval at 8× the training length with its key-value cache compressed to about 25%.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Softmax attention is the core mechanism in Transformer models, but it lacks a built-in way to forget past information. Recent work such as the Forgetting Transformer (FoX) adds a forget gate to softmax attention, down-weighting older tokens via a monotonic decay along the key-query path. However, because this decay is monotonic, it cannot selectively erase intermediate context while keeping older relevant tokens. FPA addresses this by using path extrema instead of summation, allowing non-monotonic forgetting.

## [Git Repositories Reveal Little About AI Provenance, Audit Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28655) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper audits two single-investigator repositories by the same author, one with prescriptive rule-file traces (466 commits) and one with narrative agent-scratchpad traces (103 commits), finding that 85.6% of lines ever committed to the prescriptive trace remain visible at the current revision versus only 21.8% for the narrative trace. The study also shows that the Co-Authored-By trailer, commonly used for automated AI audits, covers only 66 of 121 commits carrying any AI trailer in Project A and just 1 of 39 commits (2.6%) in Project B. As publishers and funders increasingly require disclosure of AI contributions, this audit shows that Git repositories—the natural place to look for such evidence—capture far less reliable provenance than auditors might assume. The findings challenge the adequacy of current disclosure practices and highlight the need for purpose-built provenance recording systems in AI-assisted research. The gap narrows when counted by revision: 15 of the prescriptive trace's 41 revisions leave no visible line, versus 18 of the narrative trace's 41. The paper also reports that Project A's repository-level AI trailer rate is unstable over time, stepping from 2.7% to 17.7% across an unspecified boundary, and the authors caution that neither rate can be read as an actual involvement rate.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Git is a distributed version control system that records changes to files over time, and it is widely used in computational research to track code and data. AI coding agents often leave behind two kinds of traces in a repository: prescriptive rule files (such as AGENTS.md or CLAUDE.md) that state standing instructions, and narrative scratchpads that record an agent's working notes. Provenance in this context refers to evidence of how AI tools contributed to the work, which auditors may try to reconstruct from commit history and trailers like Co-Authored-By.

## [LLM-learned checklists cut reviewer lottery error in peer review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28666) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.28666) introduces a method that learns a tree of criticism checklists from historical review corpora and selects the coarsening that maximizes a theoretically motivated measure of correlated agreement among reviewers. Tested on NeurIPS 2021 and ICLR 2022 data with simulated LLM reviewers, the learned checklist reduced the mean squared error of a three-reviewer committee by 58% on NeurIPS and 21% on ICLR, and in a 20-participant human study it lowered the MSE of paper ranking from 0.48 to 0.22. Reviewer lottery error—where a small set of reviews misses issues a larger pool would raise—undermines the fairness and reliability of scientific peer review, and this work offers a concrete, empirically validated way to mitigate it. The approach could inform how conferences design review forms and how LLM-assisted reviewing tools are built, affecting authors, reviewers, and program chairs across AI/ML venues. The method builds a tree of checklists representing coarsenings of the atomic review claim space, then selects the coarsening with the highest correlated agreement; the paper notes that issues learned directly from human reviews may be value judgments rather than verifiable properties, which is where LLM checks are most reliable. The checklist with the highest correlated agreement also had the lowest error for committees of three or more on both venues.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Peer review typically relies on a small number of reviewers per paper, so the outcome depends heavily on which reviewers are drawn—a phenomenon known as reviewer lottery error. Large language models are increasingly used to assist or simulate peer review, and checklists are a common tool to structure evaluations. This paper combines these threads by learning checklists from historical review data and validating them against real conference outcomes.

## [Paper Proposes Independent Assurance Plane for Agent-Produced Software](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28667) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper argues that when coding agents both build and verify software, their tests are no longer independent evidence, and proposes a structurally independent assurance plane with a Verification Contract. The framework separates building from verifying, using a specification-first phase without implementation access and an implementation-aware phase based on mutation analysis, property-based testing, and fuzzing, with a human making the final release decision. This work addresses a critical trust gap in AI-assisted software engineering: as coding agents increasingly self-certify their output, independent verification becomes essential to ensure reliability. The proposed framework could influence how organizations structure verification pipelines for agent-generated code, particularly in high-assurance domains. In a pre-registered study on three stateful services, a verifier in a fresh context detected more of 46 seeded faults than the builder's own tests (Holm-corrected p = 0.006), though at the cost of rare false oracles; a verifier from another provider did not improve detection. The paper characterizes four gaps (requirement, conformance, evaluation, and model gaps) and identifies which verification can close.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Coding agents are AI systems that can autonomously implement specifications, write and run tests, and declare software ready for release. Independent Verification and Validation (IV&V) is a long-standing practice in safety-critical software, where a separate team checks the software to reduce failure risk. This paper extends the IV&V principle to agent-produced software by proposing a structurally independent assurance plane.

The paper introduces VHDLBench, a benchmark and fine-tuning study that uses an 18,500+ syntax-verified VHDL dataset and a three-stage validation pipeline to evaluate five 7B-parameter LLMs for AI-assisted digital circuit design. Fine-tuned models achieved up to 92% syntax correctness and 73% functional accuracy, with DeepSeek-Coder showing the highest performance gains. This work addresses a clear gap in applying LLMs to VHDL, a less-explored hardware description language, and demonstrates practical deployment via QLoRA on 7B models, making it relevant to both AI/ML and EDA communities. It shows that domain-specific fine-tuning can enable LLMs to contribute meaningfully to generative EDA workflows. The three-level evaluation pipeline includes syntax and synthesis validation, functional simulation with testbenches, and FPGA prototyping on Zynq SoCs, confirming operational viability on real hardware. The dataset combines open-source repositories and converted Verilog designs, and fine-tuning uses parameter-efficient QLoRA for resource-constrained environments.

## [CUDA Parallelization Makes Gabriel Graph Construction Scalable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28759) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces CUDA-based parallelization strategies that reduce Gabriel graph construction time by up to 8484x compared to a sequential CPU baseline, without loss of classification accuracy. The strategies include an implicit closed-form thread-to-pair mapping, a two-pass bitmask-based edge-list construction, and thread coarsening, evaluated on 14 public benchmark datasets. Gabriel graph construction has cubic time complexity, limiting its use to small datasets; this work makes it practical at scale, enabling applications in large-margin classification and density-aware clustering. It addresses a real scalability bottleneck in geometric machine learning and high-performance computing. The GPU implementation uses an implicit closed-form mapping from linear thread index to vertex pair, eliminating the need for an explicit O(N^2) adjacency structure, and a two-pass bitmask-based atomically-compacted edge-list construction that produces a dense edge array proportional to the number of edges found. The proposed clustering method is statistically competitive with DBSCAN on the Dunn index while eliminating the need for an explicit density radius parameter.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: The Gabriel graph is a proximity graph in computational geometry where two points are connected if the closed disk with the line segment between them as diameter contains no other points. It contains the Euclidean minimum spanning tree and nearest neighbor graph as subgraphs, and is used in geometric machine learning. Naive construction compares all pairs, leading to O(N^3) complexity, which is prohibitive for large datasets.

## [SkillEval: Interpretable LLM Ability Profiles via Cognitive Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28767) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SkillEval is a new evaluation framework that uses LLMs' meta-cognitive capabilities to automatically discover interpretable skills from benchmark questions and then applies cognitive diagnosis models (CDMs) from psychometrics to estimate per-skill abilities for each model. Experiments on 3,811 LLMs across 9,523 items from five benchmarks (MATH, BBH, GPQA, MuSR, IFEval) show that SkillEval produces more calibrated and informative profiles than traditional average-based scores. Current LLM evaluation typically collapses performance into a single scalar score, which hides the diverse skills a benchmark covers and makes it hard to compare models meaningfully. SkillEval's skill-level profiles enable downstream applications such as LLM routing (cutting inference cost by over 60% while improving performance) and computerized adaptive testing (CAT), and could change how researchers and practitioners select and diagnose models. The framework combines LLM-based skill discovery with CDMs to estimate per-skill abilities, and the authors demonstrate that these profiles more accurately predict model behavior on unseen items. They also show that a trained CDM can efficiently evaluate an unseen model via CAT, and that the profiles reveal insights such as how benchmarks share skills and how instruction-tuned models differ from their base models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Cognitive diagnosis models (CDMs) are psychometric models originally developed in education to infer which specific skills or attributes a test-taker has mastered from their item responses, rather than just producing an overall score. BIG-Bench Hard (BBH) is a curated subset of 23 challenging BIG-Bench tasks that test multi-step reasoning, and benchmarks like MATH, GPQA, MuSR, and IFEval cover math, graduate-level QA, multi-step reasoning, and instruction following respectively. SkillEval borrows the CDM approach to give LLMs fine-grained, interpretable skill profiles instead of a single benchmark average.

## [ReAPO: Retry-Aware Policy Optimization for Efficient LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28770) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes ReAPO (Retry-Aware Policy Optimization), a simple modification to GRPO that reframes credit assignment for retry-based exploration as a Stochastic Shortest Path problem and allocates additional generation to failed trajectories. Across Qwen3 and OLMo-2 models (0.6B–4B) on eight reasoning benchmarks, ReAPO improves first-attempt performance over GRPO and recent baselines, and on Qwen3-0.6B it matches GRPO with twice the group size or training steps while using 39% and 31% fewer generated tokens, respectively. This work offers a theoretically principled yet lightweight fix to a known inefficiency in RLVR—repeated sampling of similar failed strategies—and could make reinforcement learning for LLM reasoning more token-efficient, which matters as training costs and exploration quality become central concerns for reasoning-model development. ReAPO introduces serial sampling by including incorrect solutions in the context for subsequent attempts, and its credit assignment ensures successful retries are reinforced without rewarding preceding failures; the approach is derived under simplifying assumptions and validated with ablations showing the utility of both serial sampling and the credit assignment scheme.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: RLVR (Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards) fine-tunes LLMs using automatic, rule-based checkers as rewards and has driven recent reasoning breakthroughs. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a popular RLVR algorithm that samples a group of answers per prompt and scores them relative to the group, but independent rollouts can repeatedly sample similar failed strategies. Stochastic Shortest Path (SSP) is a classical RL framework that minimizes expected cumulative cost to reach a terminal state, which this paper uses to formalize credit assignment across retry attempts.

## [CALLIOPE: Differentially Private Topic Discovery Without Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28797) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce CALLIOPE, a top-down framework that uses LLMs directly to discover topic hierarchies in large unstructured corpora (e.g., LLM conversations) while providing differential privacy (DP) guarantees. Unlike prior approaches that rely on clustering vector embeddings, CALLIOPE runs the entire pipeline through LLM capabilities and outperforms embedding-based methods in the DP setting, especially under strong privacy constraints. This work shows that LLMs can replace embedding-based clustering for privacy-sensitive topic discovery, potentially enabling organizations to analyze sensitive user data such as AI conversations without exposing individual records. It matters for privacy-preserving ML, where strong DP guarantees often come at a steep utility cost. CALLIOPE is evaluated using traditional clustering metrics as well as novel utility metrics introduced by the authors, and the abstract reports better utility than embedding-based methods in the high-privacy regime. The approach is top-down, meaning it recursively partitions the corpus into topic hierarchies rather than clustering flat embeddings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Differential privacy is a mathematical framework that injects calibrated noise into computations so that outputs reveal aggregate patterns but not information about any individual record. Topic modeling is a text-mining technique for discovering latent themes in document collections, and most modern methods—including LLM-augmented ones—ultimately cluster vector embeddings, which are dense numerical representations of text. CALLIOPE departs from this by having the LLM itself perform the topic discovery pipeline under DP.

<a id="item-94"></a>
## [Banzhaf Resource Attribution Reveals Coding Agents Misallocate Inference Resources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28834) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint introduces Banzhaf Resource Attribution (BRA), a controlled factorial framework that measures the marginal contribution of computation, information, and feedback to repairing failed coding agents. Evaluating six models on 194 function-level failures from MBPP+/HumanEval+ and 126 repository-level failures from SWE-bench Verified, the study finds that task-relevant specifications consistently improve repair, while public-test feedback yields small gains and retrieved code little to none. The findings expose a critical gap between the resources agents prefer and those that measurably improve repair: three of four frontier models select feedback in 95-100% of cases yet obtain lower gains than uniform random resource selection. This suggests that current inference-time scaling strategies may be misallocating token budgets, with direct implications for the design of coding agents and evaluation benchmarks. The value of additional computation declines with model capability while information remains useful, and resource channels largely exhibit diminishing returns, though complementary information can reinforce itself. The study is limited to a preprint with no community discussion and focuses on repair scenarios rather than initial code generation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Inference-time scaling refers to improving model performance by allocating more computational resources during inference, such as generating multiple samples or using feedback loops. Coding agents are LLM-based systems that autonomously modify code repositories, often evaluated on benchmarks like SWE-bench Verified for repository-level tasks and MBPP+/HumanEval+ for function-level tasks. Banzhaf attribution is a game-theoretic method for fairly distributing value among contributing factors, here adapted to measure each resource's marginal contribution to repair.

<a id="item-95"></a>
## [Gaussian Priors Structurally Hinder RL Fine-Tuning of Latent Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28849) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.28849) shows that the standard Gaussian prior on VAE latents structurally taxes mode separation and causes early collapse under RL fine-tuning. On the real alanine-dipeptide Boltzmann distribution, a Gaussian-prior VAE collapses at beta_c = 0.224 while a mode-tracked Gaussian-mixture prior collapses at 0.00316, a 71x gap from the same pipeline. This work identifies a fundamental limitation of the default Gaussian prior used in almost all latent diffusion models, showing that RL fine-tuning can amplify latent overlap and cause mode collapse. It suggests that for coverage-sensitive tasks with multimodal data, switching to a non-Gaussian prior may be necessary, impacting researchers and practitioners in generative modeling and RL fine-tuning. The gap is traced to a single geometric mediator, d_min/(2 sigma_0), and verified through controlled interventions: moving geometry at fixed prior and switching prior at matched geometry (full mediation; prior-label coefficient 0.995x, 95% CI [0.79, 1.25]). A three-source decomposition localizes the amplification to the learned policy, with zero ODE-solver contribution and bounded anchor mismatch.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Latent diffusion models typically use a variational autoencoder (VAE) to compress data into a latent space, and they almost universally place a standard Gaussian prior on that latent space. The VAE is trained with a KL divergence term that encourages the latent distribution to match this prior, but this term does not account for multimodal structure in the data. Reinforcement learning fine-tuning is often used to align diffusion models with downstream objectives, but it can lead to mode collapse, where the model loses diversity and generates only a few modes.

<a id="item-96"></a>
## [DualHeads aligns few-step diffusion models with binary classifier heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28858) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DualHeads introduces a simple method to align distilled few-step diffusion models with human preferences using two lightweight binary classifier heads on a frozen diffusion backbone, avoiding differentiable rewards or stochastic trajectories. The reward head learns which samples of the same prompt score higher, the reference head separates reference images from generated ones, and the generator maximizes a weighted sum of their logits. This approach enables few-step generators to achieve task rewards close to those of 40-step reinforcement learning methods while preserving image quality, at no extra inference cost. It offers a practical alignment path for distilled models that lack score fields or stochastic trajectories, benefiting the diffusion model community. When the heads are optimal, the objective is KL-regularized maximization of the learned reward, and the ratio of head weights sets the regularization strength. Empirically, with both rule-based and model-based rewards, a few-step DualHeads generator reaches task rewards close to those of 40-step reinforcement learning methods while preserving image quality.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Distilled diffusion models generate high-quality images in a few sampling steps by compressing multi-step diffusion models, but aligning them with task requirements and human preferences is difficult. Methods developed for multi-step diffusion models rely on differentiable rewards, or on score fields and stochastic trajectories that distilled generators may lack. DualHeads asks whether a few-step generator can be adapted using only external reward evaluations and reference samples, and shows that two binary classifiers suffice.
