Contrastive decoding is a training-free text generation technique that contrasts output distributions under different conditions to produce more coherent and factual outputs; context-aware decoding (CAD) is a variant that amplifies the difference between outputs with and without context to reduce hallucination.

## [Stochastic Pruning of DPO Computation Graph Speeds Training 1.4x](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29538) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.29538) introduces a unified framework for stochastic pruning of the Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) computation graph, independently sampling response positions, prefixes, and preference branches to omit computation at each training step. The method achieves a 1.426x step-time speedup over a concatenated DPO baseline and reduces training time by about 30% relative to stock DPO across two model families, with slightly lower mean accuracy. DPO is a widely used alignment technique for large language models, and reducing its training cost without introducing bias could make preference tuning more accessible and scalable. This work provides a principled way to trade computation for slight accuracy changes, which is valuable for ML efficiency researchers and practitioners training large models under resource constraints. The framework uses inverse-probability weighting to obtain a conditionally unbiased single-branch gradient estimator for the sampled objective. Position and sequence pruning primarily reduce peak memory, with most output-memory savings coming from an implementation that retains full response supervision, while branch pruning yields the step-time speedup but slightly lower mean accuracy and benchmark-dependent changes in open-ended scores.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is a method for aligning language models with human preferences without needing a separate reward model or reinforcement learning, making it simpler and more stable than RLHF. However, DPO still incurs significant computation in scoring outputs, executing sequences, and backpropagating gradients, and simply reducing supervision does not necessarily save computation. Stochastic pruning is a technique that randomly omits parts of a computation graph during training while maintaining unbiased gradient estimates, often using inverse-probability weighting to correct for the omitted parts.

## [Reflexivity Matrix Sets Stability Threshold for Multi-Agent Performative Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29577) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a reflexivity matrix Γ that captures cross-agent feedback in multi-agent performative prediction, and identifies a spectral threshold at spectral radius ρ(Γ)=1 that separates stable learning from unstable learning. Below the threshold, repeated retraining converges geometrically to a unique performatively stable equilibrium; above it, the authors construct noisy supercritical instances where cumulative excess loss grows linearly. This work provides a finer-grained stability analysis for multi-agent learning systems by accounting for network structure, which prior scalar contraction constants miss. It could impact the design and deployment of interacting machine learning models where feedback loops affect data distributions. The reflexivity matrix is defined from per-agent gradient sensitivities and curvature parameters, and the threshold recovers the single-agent scalar threshold as a special case. The supercritical lower bound holds for horizons up to order 1/(ρ(Γ)-1), showing linear growth of excess loss.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Performative prediction refers to settings where a model's predictions influence the data distribution it is trained on, creating a feedback loop. In multi-agent settings, multiple learners interact through a network, so stability depends on the pattern of interactions, not just the overall strength of performativity. Prior work often simplified this by using a scalar contraction constant, ignoring network structure.

## [Legendre Polynomials Enable Derivative-Based Training of Stochastic Flow Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29605) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes representing Brownian paths with shifted Legendre polynomials and a Wong-Zakai styled objective to enable derivative-based training of stochastic flow maps for few-step SDE sampling. It also provides a recipe for fine-tuning large pre-trained flow models into strong stochastic flow maps, achieving roughly a three-fold improvement in FD-DINOv2 over SSFM at four NFE on ImageNet. This addresses a key obstacle in few-step SDE sampling by making derivative-based losses applicable to stochastic flow maps, which could significantly improve generative modeling and simulation. The method enables efficient training of strong stochastic flow maps that outperform previous approaches. The approach identifies two core design principles: representing the Brownian path with a basis carrying higher-order local information, and training with an objective that forces the network to use the full representation, not just the first order. The shifted Legendre polynomial expansion with a Wong-Zakai styled objective unlocks derivative-based losses, and the method is ablated on toy problems before scaling to ImageNet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Stochastic flow maps aim to enable few-step sampling of stochastic differential equations (SDEs) by learning to directly jump between times along the solution path. However, training these models is difficult because the Brownian path is infinite-dimensional and nowhere differentiable, making derivative-based losses hard to implement. Shifted Legendre polynomials are a complete orthogonal basis often used to approximate Brownian motion, and the Wong-Zakai approximation is a technique for approximating SDEs driven by rough paths with ordinary differential equations.

Researchers developed dLI-RADS, an interpretable AI method that combines neural network predictions with handcrafted quantitative imaging features reflecting LI-RADS major features via logistic regression to estimate hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) probability from multiphase CT. In a multicenter retrospective study, dLI-RADS achieved 80% lesion-level accuracy versus 74% for LI-RADS on the internal evaluation set, demonstrating non-inferiority (p=0.04). This work shows that an AI system can remain aligned with the clinically standard LI-RADS framework while improving diagnostic accuracy, potentially aiding radiologists in consistently assessing small liver lesions where visual feature evaluation is challenging. It represents a step toward trustworthy clinical translation of medical AI for early HCC detection. The study included patients with multiphase CT and at least one liver lesion, excluding lesions larger than 40 mm, and used histology as the reference standard for HCC with a composite reference standard for non-HCC lesions. Non-inferiority was tested with 10,000-repetition bootstrap tests at a 5% margin, and external test set sensitivities were also compared.

## [Position Paper Maps Recursive Self-Improvement for Physical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29677) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv position paper (2610.29677) brings the concept of recursive self-improvement (RSI) into robot learning, tracing pathways from improving robot capabilities to improving the processes that generate those capabilities. It identifies four conditions under which gains carry forward and notes that reviewed physical studies have yet to isolate gains attributable to recursion itself. RSI is a central concept in debates about AI takeoff and safety, but it has been studied almost exclusively in digital agents that rewrite their own code. Extending it to embodied systems highlights new constraints—partial observability, costly trials, and irreversible physical consequences—that could shape how safely and effectively self-improving robots are developed. The paper argues that gains carry forward only when feedback addresses remaining learning needs, the receiving component can actually use the change, its value survives changes in tasks or hardware, and continued practice remains safe and affordable. It synthesizes existing manipulation-focused studies into evaluation principles and research directions, while acknowledging that no reviewed physical system has yet isolated recursion-specific gains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Recursive self-improvement describes a system that improves not only its behavior but also the process by which it improves, potentially leading to rapid capability gains. Recent digital agents test this by rewriting their own code and validating each version with executable tests. Robot learning, by contrast, must learn from partial observations through costly physical trials, while operating conditions change and mistakes can have irreversible consequences.

## [Scaling Multilingual Pixel Language Models: Local Gains and Capacity Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29681) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper trains a family of MPIXEL models along three axes—more languages, more scripts, and larger model capacity under fixed per-language compute—and evaluates them on five tasks covering 83 languages. It finds that scaling acts locally: each axis improves performance on the added languages and scripts, while increased capacity mitigates the curse of multilinguality from pretraining on more languages. This work provides systematic empirical insights into how multilingual scaling affects pixel-based language models, showing they outperform subword and character-level baselines on typologically distant low-resource languages across many writing systems. These findings are valuable for multilingual NLP and multimodal pretraining, especially for extending language coverage to low-resource and unseen scripts. Transfer to unseen scripts improves with multilingual pretraining but does not further improve with scaling, and cross-lingual alignment weakens as more languages are added. Larger pixel models also reserve more layers for visual processing, suggesting a capacity-dependent shift in how text is represented.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Pixel language models process text as rendered images rather than through a fixed vocabulary, which gives them strong cross-lingual transfer abilities based on visual similarity of writing systems. The curse of multilinguality refers to the phenomenon where adding more languages to a single model degrades per-language performance under fixed capacity. This paper studies how scaling along languages, scripts, and model size affects these trade-offs.

## [Spiking Ternary GRU with Resonate-and-Fire Neurons Achieves Single-Digit WER on LibriSpeech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29720) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the Spiking Ternary GRU (stGRU), which couples a ternary-weight gated recurrent unit for temporal filtering with downstream Resonate-and-Fire (RF) neurons that natively generate binary spike streams. Trained end-to-end on LibriSpeech-960, the Small, Medium, and Large stGRUs achieve 8.09%, 7.04%, and 6.44±0.17% WER on test-clean without an external language model. This is claimed to be the first matrix-MAC-free spiking network without a learned ANN sequence decoder to achieve single-digit WER on LibriSpeech, addressing a key bottleneck of Leaky Integrate-and-Fire neurons in continuous speech recognition. It could advance energy-efficient neuromorphic ASR and inspire bio-inspired architectures that natively produce binary events. Binary event transmission is enforced as a native training constraint rather than a post-hoc activation quantization step, and ternary weights plus learned projections avoid dense floating-point matrix MACs. The architecture also achieves 10.88% test CER on Aishell-1 (9.28% on dev) when trained from scratch, and trained networks develop a depth-graded hierarchy with shallow layers acting as fast acoustic filters and deep layers as slower semantic integrators.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) promise energy-efficient inference through sparse, event-driven computation, but they struggle on continuous, context-dependent tasks like automatic speech recognition (ASR). A key bottleneck is the Leaky Integrate-and-Fire (LIF) neuron, whose membrane decay erases information across pauses and phonetic boundaries while it must both extract features and retain long-range context. Resonate-and-Fire (RF) neurons are a spiking neuron model with intrinsic subthreshold oscillatory dynamics, giving them frequency selectivity distinct from classical integrate-and-fire models. Ternary weights use three discrete values, reducing memory and compute compared to floating-point weights.

## [SAKURA streams compressed attention KV state to phones for private image editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29733) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SAKURA introduces a cloud-edge architecture that delivers reusable attention key-value (KV) state to mobile devices under a bandwidth budget, using token-importance-aware compression and a pipelined runtime that overlaps transfer, reconstruction, and on-device execution. Evaluated on a Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 phone across three editing tasks and two diffusion models, it reduces latency by 1.82x over CacheGen adapted to DiT and 3.27x over on-device generation, while cutting cache storage by 3.06x with preserved image quality. This work tackles a practical bottleneck for privacy-preserving on-device image editing: reusable KV state is too large to store on phones, but transferring it over fluctuating mobile networks can be slower than local computation. By making cloud-edge KV streaming bandwidth-adaptive, SAKURA could enable private, low-latency diffusion-transformer editing services on commodity smartphones. SAKURA uses fine-grained token-importance-aware compression to preserve quality under limited transfer budgets and adaptively selects the cache representation for each upcoming shard based on current network bandwidth. Its pipelined runtime overlaps cache transfer and reconstruction with on-device execution, and the evaluation covers three editing tasks and two diffusion models on a Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 phone.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion transformers are widely used in image-editing services, and cloud-device collaboration is common to balance efficiency and privacy. Many editing workloads, such as face swapping and virtual try-on, use common templates, so attention key-value (KV) state can be precomputed and reused across requests. However, this reusable state is large and scales with the number of templates, making full on-device storage impractical, while cloud storage puts cache transfer on the inference path.

## [MeMoDecode Enforces Metamodel Consistency in LLM-Generated DSLs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29749) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present MeMoDecode, a decoding framework that incrementally maps LLM output to partial DSL models and verifies metamodel-level consistency during generation, jointly enforcing concrete syntax, abstract syntax, and well-formedness constraints. Across four DSLs and six open-weight LLMs (0.6B–70B parameters), it improves consistency by 23% on average over unconstrained decoding and 8% over grammar-constrained decoding. This work addresses a key limitation of existing constrained decoding methods, which typically guarantee only concrete syntax compliance, by enforcing metamodel-level semantic constraints during generation. It could significantly improve the reliability of LLM-based code generation for domain-specific languages in model-driven engineering workflows. MeMoDecode uses a retry mechanism that resumes decoding from the most recent completable snapshot when an inconsistency is detected, and under a sound backend verifier it never performs worse than grammar-constrained decoding on alignment metrics. The approach incurs only modest runtime overhead while improving benchmark performance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Domain-specific languages (DSLs) are defined by a metamodel that specifies abstract syntax (domain concepts and relations) and well-formedness constraints, plus a concrete syntax for textual representation. Constrained decoding guides LLM generation to satisfy formal constraints, but prior methods typically enforce only concrete syntax or limited semantic constraints expressed over text, making it hard to guarantee metamodel-level consistency.

## [LLMs' AI Recommendation Bias Traced to Pretraining and Instruction Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29785) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.29785) shows that pretrained LLMs already include explicit AI/ML terms in 27% of their recommendations, rising to 59% after instruction tuning. Controlled fine-tuning experiments reveal that removing the 7% of instruction examples concerning AI cuts the rate by 14 percentage points, while data rewarding helpfulness raises it by 9 points and data ranked by diverse simulated user preferences lowers it by 9 points. This work pinpoints the origins of a subtle but pervasive bias in LLM recommendations, showing that data curation and reward design during post-training can mitigate it. It has implications for AI alignment, data curation practices, and the design of recommendation systems that rely on LLMs. The study also finds that question phrasing matters: asking for the "best" option or what to do "right now" raises the AI recommendation rate by 8 and 18 points respectively, while telling the model it is an AI advisor adds 4 points. These effects are observed with models held fixed, highlighting the interplay between learned tendencies and prompt framing.

## [Study questions designed agent memory as models grow stronger](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29823) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A short arXiv empirical study, 'MemoryTax', tested four memory methods (full context, BM25, Mem0, Zep) and three coding agents (Pi, Codex, Claude Code) across three models (GPT-4o-mini, GPT-6 Luna, Claude Opus 5.5) on one 419-turn LoCoMo conversation with 152 questions. It found Mem0 beats full context only with GPT-4o-mini, and that with Claude Opus 5.5, Pi reached 99% accuracy at less than half the cost of full context. The results suggest that as foundation models become capable enough to search raw history with their own tools, elaborate memory pipelines like Mem0 and Zep may become costly overhead rather than necessary infrastructure. This echoes the 'bitter lesson' in AI: general methods that scale with compute tend to outperform hand-engineered solutions, which could reshape how agent memory frameworks are designed and marketed. The study is limited: it uses only one conversation and one run per cell, so generalizability is weak. It also notes that agent memory has a ceiling set by the model that writes it—a stronger reader cannot recover what a weaker writer omitted—and that part of Pi's lead came from photo captions only the agents could read, with Pi and full context differing by just one answer on the 88 questions whose evidence has no photo.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Agent memory systems like Mem0 and Zep pre-process conversation history to decide what to store and retrieve, aiming to reduce token costs and improve relevance. In contrast, coding agents keep the full raw history and let the model search it with tools such as BM25, a classic information-retrieval ranking function. The 'bitter lesson' refers to the observation that AI approaches leveraging increased computation consistently outperform those relying on human-designed heuristics.

## [Robot Generalization Needs Data Diversity, Not Just Volume](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29829) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.29829v2) argues that zero-shot generalization in robotics is better achieved through diverse data from many contributors rather than large volumes from a few collectors. It proposes a shared latent action space combining multi-modal alignment, generative augmentation, and noisy-label learning, and suggests a community-driven data collection model built around a standard robot called Origami. This reframes zero-shot generalization as coverage of the deployment distribution, challenging the dominant paid-collection paradigm in robotics and potentially enabling more scalable, crowd-sourced data pipelines for physical AI. If validated, it could lower the barrier for smaller teams to contribute to and benefit from general-purpose robot learning. The paper shows that additional samples from the same collectors shrink the unseen region only slowly, while each new contributor can extend coverage, and it closes with a testable hypothesis and an evaluation protocol linking real-robot zero-shot success to contributed data diversity. As an arXiv preprint, it has not yet undergone peer review or community discussion.

In digital AI, zero-shot capability grew with the volume and diversity of training data, much of it crowd-sourced, whereas robotics still relies on paid collection by a few well-funded teams. A latent action space is a compact, semantically structured representation that enables efficient policy learning and cross-embodiment transfer, and multi-modal alignment helps map different sensory modalities into a common representation.

## [S-EDL calibrates LLMs for open-ended generation via sequence-likelihood evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29832) ⭐️ 7.0/10

S-EDL introduces an evidential method that elicits evidence from a model's own sequence likelihoods to construct a differentiable Dirichlet prior over prompt-specific semantic classes, enabling calibration-aware training without fixed multiple-choice options. Evaluated on four open-ended benchmarks and three models, it substantially reduces semantic calibration error while improving accuracy. Most calibration techniques assume fixed-set classification or binary correctness, which breaks down when many different token sequences express the same meaning; S-EDL directly targets this gap for open-ended generation. By aligning generative likelihoods with semantic correctness, it offers a path toward more trustworthy LLM deployment in complex, free-form applications. The method optimizes an evidential loss under a Dirichlet prior derived from sequence likelihoods, yielding a training signal over a variable number of semantic classes rather than a fixed label set. Code is released at github.com/sandylaker/s-edl, and the approach is evaluated across four open-ended benchmarks and three models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Calibration refers to how well a model's confidence matches its actual correctness; a well-calibrated LLM that says it is 80% sure should be right about 80% of the time. In open-ended generation, the same meaning can be expressed by many different token sequences, so string-level or multiple-choice calibration is ill-suited. Evidential deep learning models uncertainty by treating predictions as evidence for a Dirichlet distribution, a natural conjugate prior for categorical outcomes. S-EDL combines these ideas by using sequence likelihoods as evidence to build a prompt-specific Dirichlet prior for semantic classes.

## [PAUSE: Search-Free Steering of Coding Agents Before Their First Source-Code Edit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29870) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PAUSE introduces a search-free framework that intercepts a coding agent's first source-code edit and injects a single planner-generated repair plan to improve patch success. Evaluated on SWE-bench Verified and Multilingual, it improves resolution by up to 16.8% online and recovers 35.4% of eligible failed tasks offline. This approach offers a lightweight alternative to costly periodic monitoring or checkpoint search for steering coding agents, potentially reducing token costs and improving success rates in real-world software engineering tasks. It is relevant to developers and researchers working on LLM-based coding agents and automated program repair. PAUSE requires no learned monitor, no search over checkpoints, and no extra model call beyond the Planner, and it works both online during a live run and offline from a recorded trajectory prefix. It outperforms LivePlan by 3.2-3.7% at similar Planner cost and is comparable to SICA's periodic overseer at about 11-15% of its Planner cost, while resuming from a recorded prefix uses 33-41% fewer Executor tokens than restarting.

**Background**: Coding agents are LLM-powered systems that autonomously explore repositories, reproduce bugs, and generate patches to fix issues. Existing online steering methods periodically review agent trajectories or invoke an advisor when a monitor detects problems, but frequent reviews are costly and the absence of anomalies does not guarantee correct progress. PAUSE instead targets the first source-code edit, which agents typically reach at a median of 60% of trajectory progress, to inject a single repair plan from a separate Planner model.

## [Value-Specific Fine-Tuning Causes Unintended LLM Side Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29922) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.29922) adapts four instruction-tuned LLMs across individual Hofstede value dimensions and finds that value-specific training produces unintended cross-value transfer and behavioral side effects on untrained values, risk preferences, social cognition, and safety traits. This work shows that narrow value alignment evaluations miss important spillover effects, suggesting that post-training pipelines should incorporate broader behavioral assessments to avoid degrading safety or other capabilities when instilling specific values. The study evaluates four instruction-tuned models on both cross-value transfer and broader behaviors, revealing that fine-tuning on one Hofstede dimension can alter unrelated traits; the authors call for investigating where additional value training best fits in the pipeline.

## [Position Paper Argues LLM Privacy Must Be Context-Specific, Not One-Size-Fits-All](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29925) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv position paper (2610.29925) argues that the trend toward a unified prompt-privacy framework for general-purpose LLMs is misleading and can create a false sense of security. The authors recommend three paths forward: application-specific LLM deployment, context-specific evaluation of privacy frameworks, and better privacy literacy. As users interact with a single general-purpose LLM across many distinct contexts, a unified privacy solution could fail to protect sensitive information in specific settings such as healthcare or finance. This paper challenges researchers and practitioners to rethink privacy design and evaluation, which could influence how LLM applications are deployed and regulated. The paper is a position paper without empirical results, and it argues that even future unified frameworks must confront fundamental tensions among desirable privacy properties. It emphasizes that privacy is inherently contextual, drawing on decades of privacy research, and calls for context-aware evaluation rather than one-size-fits-all metrics.

**Background**: Contextual integrity is a privacy theory that defines privacy as appropriate information flow according to the norms of a specific context, rather than as secrecy or control. ## [Benchmarks as Empirical Measures Over Difficulty: New Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29927) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper reframes benchmark evaluation as an empirical measure over difficulty, formalizing two failure modes—measure mismatch and coverage gaps—and proposes two diagnostic metrics with theoretical guarantees. It analyzes 25 benchmarks across 7 domains and introduces BenchFill, a difficulty-targeted item rewriting algorithm to reduce coverage gaps. This work provides a principled framework for diagnosing benchmark quality, showing that many popular benchmarks implicitly overweight certain difficulty regions or leave gaps, which can mislead leaderboard conclusions and limit monitoring of model progress. It affects ML researchers and practitioners who rely on benchmarks for model selection and evaluation. The diagnostic metrics come with rigorous theoretical guarantees, and item difficulties are carefully estimated. BenchFill is a simple difficulty-targeted item rewriting algorithm that effectively reduces local coverage gaps, turning diagnosis into action.

## [HiddenLens: A Six-Layer BERT-LLM Pipeline for Hidden Assumptions in Requirements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29996) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose HiddenLens, a six-layer BERT-LLM pipeline that detects, classifies, questions, and resolves hidden assumptions in natural-language software requirements, and introduce the HiddenLens Corpus (HLC) with 312 requirements annotated with 847 hidden assumptions across six non-functional requirement categories. HiddenLens outperforms keyword-based and QuARS-Adapted baselines by 71% and 61%, respectively, achieving an end-to-end F1-score of 0.79, while its BERT-based NFR classifier achieves an F1-score of 0.847. Hidden assumptions in requirements can lead to costly rework if discovered late, and this work is among the first to address them systematically rather than merely flagging ambiguity. The framework and annotated corpus could help requirements analysts and software teams resolve assumptions early, improving architecture, testing, and deployment outcomes. The pipeline uses BERT attention entropy to localize unresolved spans, chain-of-thought prompting to articulate assumptions and failure risks, a fine-tuned BERT classifier for six NFR categories (performance, security, scalability, usability, reliability, domain), few-shot LLM prompting to generate ranked clarification options, human validation, and requirement rewriting. The HLC corpus is inspired by PROMISE and PURE and is claimed to be the first dataset specifically developed for hidden assumption detection.

## [Open Generator Creates Telecom Alarm Storms with Exact Root Causes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30004) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces an open generator for telecom alarm storms that records the exact root cause and propagation path of every alarm, with all fields bound to NORIA-O, RFC 8632, RFC 8345, or TMF642. It also shows that a simple rule ranking the device with the earliest critical alarm achieves top-1 accuracy of 0.650, outperforming a temporal method (0.575) and an LLM (0.600). Telecom operator logs are confidential, so shareable data for alarm root-cause analysis is scarce; this generator enables reproducible research by providing synthetic data with exact labels. The finding that a simple baseline outperforms more complex methods highlights the importance of strong baselines and may influence future AIOps research. The generator runs on real backbone topologies from the Internet Topology Zoo and ships with fixed configurations, splits, a scorer, and nine baselines. A key limitation is that when a site loses power and its PoP reports nothing, methods that rank alarmed devices score near zero, while topology-aware methods achieve high accuracy.

## [Agent Behavior Maps Locate LLM Agents Without Running Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30014) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Agent Behavior Maps, a method that represents LLM-harness combinations using centered action log-likelihoods on shared reference trajectories, eliminating the need for new environment rollouts from the target agent. The authors derive error bounds linking squared coordinate distances to trajectory KL divergence, and validate the approach across 100 LLMs and four environments, including ALFWorld. This work enables performance prediction and behavioral similarity analysis for LLM agents without costly environment rollouts, which could significantly speed up agent evaluation and model comparison. It offers a new lens for understanding how different models and harnesses behave, beyond simple success rates. The method's distances track directly measured trajectory KL in rank order across eight cross-scored models, and in ALFWorld, behavioral similarities persist after controlling for success rates and model size, even across model families. As an extension, harness-component displacements approximately compose, though accuracy varies by component and environment.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are systems where a language model interacts with an environment through a harness that manages tools, memory, and action parsing. Evaluating such agents typically requires running them in environments like ALFWorld, a text-based embodied benchmark, which can be expensive and time-consuming. Agent Behavior Maps instead use action log-likelihoods—the model's probability of taking specific actions—on reference trajectories to characterize behavior without execution.

## [Code Comprehensor: Hybrid Retrieval Framework for Large Codebases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30016) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Code Comprehensor is a hybrid retrieval-reasoning framework that combines a code dependency callgraph (CodeGraph), hierarchical LLM-generated documentation (CodeScribe), and a CLIP-style dual-encoder (CodeContrast) to enable fast, semantically precise retrieval of code contexts for LLM-based question answering. Evaluated on OpenCV and a proprietary 5M-LOC repository, it achieves above 90% faithfulness scores in RAGAS LLM-as-a-judge evaluation. This work addresses a core challenge in software engineering at scale: understanding large, complex, and rapidly evolving codebases, which hinders onboarding, maintenance, refactoring, and impact analysis. By providing grounded evidence to LLMs through cross-modal retrieval, it could significantly improve developer productivity and code comprehension tools. The framework uses CodeScribe to generate hierarchical documentation across repository call graphs, which supervises CodeContrast to learn aligned text-code embeddings. Richer textual supervision—including hierarchical documentation, parent/sibling context, and LLM-generated synthetic questions—consistently enhances retrieval performance across both repositories.

**Background**: A call graph is a static representation of possible program execution paths, showing which functions call which others. ## [Stationarity Constraints Improve Machine-Learned Exchange-Correlation Functionals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30022) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers show that penalizing the Brillouin residual during training of a neural meta-GGA exchange-correlation functional improves both self-consistent energies and densities, outperforming SCAN on unseen molecules. The functional was trained on 6 energies and 11 CCSD densities without requiring self-consistent-field solutions during training. This addresses the known trade-off between energy and density accuracy in machine-learned functionals, which often achieve good energies but poor densities when used self-consistently. The approach could lead to more reliable density functional theory calculations for computational chemistry and materials science without expensive self-consistent training. The Brillouin residual on Kohn-Sham orbitals of an accurate density is shown to control, to first order, both the self-consistent density shift and the gap between fitted and self-consistent energies. Penalizing this residual requires no self-consistent-field solution during training, making the approach computationally efficient.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Density functional theory (DFT) is a widely used computational method for electronic structure, but its accuracy depends on the exchange-correlation functional. Empirical functionals are typically fitted on fixed orbitals but then used self-consistently, leading to a mismatch between fitted and self-consistent results. Meta-GGA functionals like SCAN include additional ingredients such as the kinetic energy density and are considered state-of-the-art for many properties.

## [Document converters silently drop formatting cues, making enterprise agents confidently wrong](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30026) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.30026) shows that every mainstream document converter, including docling and plain-text extraction, silently discards author-intended formatting cues such as highlight and colour. On a benchmark of 76 real policy documents, formatting-blind ingestion collapses micro F1 from 0.690 to 0.150 (docling) and 0.075 (plain text), a gap that replicates across six answering models from two vendors. The finding reveals that reliability for enterprise agents is won or lost at the ingestion layer, not the reasoning layer: agents become confidently wrong rather than visibly incomplete, which is especially dangerous for compliance and policy workflows. It also highlights a blind spot in parsing benchmarks, since colour—the cue no mainstream converter preserves—is common, appearing in 32.8% of 6,460 documents. A span-level leave-one-out analysis localises the loss to two cues—highlight (+0.302) and colour (+0.262), both Holm-adjusted p < 1e-4—while bold, italic, monospace and strikethrough each sit within ±0.003 of zero. A pre-registered test shows that explicitly typing a cue for the agent adds +0.085 beyond merely preserving it (Holm-adjusted p = 0.049), and labels were validated against rendered page images with κ = 0.72.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Enterprise agents often work from Word files or PDFs, and before an agent can follow a policy or answer a compliance question, an ingestion layer must convert the document into tokens. Office Open XML (OOXML) is Microsoft's zipped, XML-based format for Word documents that stores formatting properties, while tools like docling convert PDFs and Office files into structured data for AI. Micro F1 is a standard evaluation metric that aggregates precision and recall across all classes, and here it measures how well an agent recovers the requirements an author singled out.

SP-HLM introduces a sparse-pose-driven interface that combines five task-critical body poses with a learned latent motion prior, where a vision-language-action model (SP-VLA) predicts sparse poses and finger joints, and a distilled motion generation model (SP-MGM) reconstructs full-body motion in real time. Experiments in simulation and on a real humanoid robot show improved motion tracking, whole-body coordination, and loco-manipulation success over existing approaches. This work addresses a key challenge in humanoid loco-manipulation by decoupling high-level task intent from whole-body coordination, potentially reducing the burden on high-level policies and enabling more robust real-time control. It could influence the design of interfaces for generalist humanoid robots that need to perform complex mobile manipulation tasks. The framework uses asynchronous action-chunk inference in SP-VLA and a two-stage distillation process for SP-MGM, with a pretrained motion encoder mapping motion to a compact latent representation that is integrated by a general motion tracker (SP-GMT). The approach is validated in both simulation and real-world settings, though the paper is an arXiv preprint and impact is not yet peer-reviewed.

## [Parallel AI agent collaboration modeled as graph-structured block optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30074) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.30074) models parallel AI agent collaboration as discrete block optimization over a dependency graph, proving that an optimal fractional coloring yields a merger preserving a 1/χ_f(G) fraction of total verified local improvement, with geometric convergence under conditional local-improvement and block error-bound conditions. This provides a theoretical foundation for coordinating multiple AI agents that make local edits to a shared solution, with guarantees on solution quality and convergence, which could improve reliability of multi-agent systems in code repair, routing, and distributed optimization. The guarantee is tight among mergers selecting a subset of the same proposals; the convergence rate separates task structure, agent allocation, and computation cost, yielding a linear program for agent assignment and an explicit time-cost trade-off. Experiments show verified merging resolves conflicts in code repair, and on road networks fractional integration retains 922 units of graph-cut improvement across 90 paired runs, while fractional scheduling reduces wall time by a median of 15% across 12 paired runs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Fractional coloring is a graph theory concept where vertices are assigned sets of colors, and the fractional chromatic number is the minimum ratio of colors to set size, computable via linear programming but NP-hard in general. Discrete block optimization deals with optimizing over discrete variables grouped into blocks, often with dependency constraints. Geometric convergence means the objective gap decreases exponentially with iterations, a desirable property in distributed optimization.

## [AtomicRL: Atomic Reinforcement Learning for Long-Horizon Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30097) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AtomicRL is a new RL post-training framework for vision-language-action (VLA) models that uses atomic task structures to improve credit assignment and exploration in long-horizon robotic manipulation. It introduces Semantic-Aware Policy Optimization (SAPO) and Bottleneck-Adaptive Target Exploration (BATE), and reports gains of 3.6% and 4.0% over PPO on LIBERO-LONG, up to 9.5% on CALVIN ABC-D, and an average 21.3% improvement in real-world tasks via a Real2Sim2Real pipeline. Long-horizon manipulation is a key bottleneck for deploying VLA models in real-world robotics, and this work directly addresses the delayed and biased credit assignment that plagues trajectory-level sparse-reward RL. If validated, the framework could make RL post-training of VLA policies more sample-efficient and practical for physical robots. SAPO uses semantic-aware gating to prevent TD residuals from failed atomic subtasks from propagating across task boundaries, mitigating credit contamination while preserving intra-subtask credit assignment and value bootstrapping. BATE dynamically coordinates task sampling and local exploration incentives based on per-subtask mastery, and the Real2Sim2Real pipeline bridges online RL in simulation with real-world deployment.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models extend pretrained vision-language models with action outputs and are typically trained by imitation learning on robot demonstrations. Reinforcement learning can further improve such policies, but sparse trajectory-level rewards make credit assignment difficult—especially in long-horizon tasks where success depends on many subtasks. Temporal-difference (TD) learning, which updates value estimates by bootstrapping from subsequent predictions, is the foundation of many RL algorithms but can propagate errors across subtask boundaries.

## [Differentially Private Sparse Reward Estimation for RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30099) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the first theoretical framework for differentially private sparse reward estimation from pairwise preference feedback under the Bradley-Terry-Luce model, with algorithms for both Sample-DP and Label-DP settings. It establishes upper bounds of Õ(s*/n + s*²/(n²ε²)) for Sample-DP and Õ(s*/n + s*/(nε²)) for Label-DP, depending on the sparse feature dimension s* rather than the full model size d. Existing private alignment methods assume dense reward parameterizations, leading to error bounds that scale polynomially with the model size d, which is prohibitive for modern overparameterized LLMs. This work shows that by assuming sparsity (s* << d), one can achieve much tighter privacy-utility trade-offs, making private RLHF more practical for large models. The theoretical results include lower bounds showing that the Sample-DP rate is nearly optimal and that the Label-DP guarantee cannot be improved within the randomized-response framework. Empirically, the framework outperforms dense baselines on sentiment generation and safety alignment benchmarks, but the abstract does not provide detailed experimental setups or specific numbers.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement Learning with Human Feedback (RLHF) is a key technique for aligning large language models with human preferences, but it relies on sensitive preference data, necessitating differential privacy (DP) guarantees to protect user information. The Bradley-Terry-Luce model is a standard probabilistic model for pairwise comparisons, often used to model human preferences in RLHF. Sparse estimation assumes that only a small subset of features (s* out of d) are relevant, which is common in overparameterized models. Differential privacy provides a formal guarantee that the output of an algorithm does not reveal much about any single data point.

<a id="item-171"></a>
## [ASTEROID: Distilling Privileged Experts for On-Policy Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30105) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ASTEROID is a student-teacher supervised learning framework that learns test-time exploration by distilling a privileged expert's actions conditioned on the student's on-policy history under partial observability. Across eleven environments, it achieves roughly 2x higher returns under fixed exploration budgets and uses about 100x fewer interactions than reinforcement learning baselines. This work offers a sample-efficient alternative to online reinforcement learning for learning exploration, which could benefit robotics, navigation, and other sequential decision-making domains where interaction is costly. It also shows that a history-conditioned student can learn to explore even when its teacher never needs to, addressing a key limitation of offline imitation under partial observability. The student first collects on-policy context under partial observability, and the privileged expert then provides action labels conditioned on that context, enabling behavior similar to Bayesian posterior sampling: search while uncertain and exploit once the hidden state is revealed. The method also transfers from simulation to vision-denied real-world robot manipulation, improving success by +40% over standard distillation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: In partially observable environments, agents must gather information about hidden state before they can act well, a behavior known as exploration. Online reinforcement learning can learn exploration from scratch but is sample-inefficient, while offline imitation learning averages expert actions over hidden states and therefore fails to capture information-gathering. Privileged imitation learning uses a teacher that observes the full state, but the student may be unable to imitate it due to partial observability, a problem known as teacher-student asymmetry.

## [PAPAV: A Unified Five-Capability Framework for Multimodal Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30108) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This arXiv paper introduces PAPAV, a unified capability-centric framework of five recurring functions—Perceive, Anticipate, Plan, Act, and Verify—for analyzing multimodal embodied agents (MMEAs) and contrasting computer-use multimodal agents (MMAs) with robotic systems (RSs). Across 64 benchmarks, the authors find that explicit evaluation centers on Act, with only 5 benchmarks assessing Anticipate and 3 assessing Verify. The framework offers a shared conceptual vocabulary for comparing computer-use agents and embodied robots, which could help researchers design more reliable physical agents and measure progress across all five capabilities rather than relying on task success alone. It is particularly relevant to the embodied AI and agent-architecture communities as multimodal agents increasingly move from screens into the physical world. PAPAV is defined by function rather than architecture, and the paper identifies five physical constraints—ranging from partial observability to unverifiable outcomes—that leave fewer choices fixed in advance and less room to reverse errors in robotic settings. The work is a conceptual survey/position paper rather than an empirical breakthrough, and its benchmark analysis reveals that Anticipate and Verify remain largely hidden behind task-success metrics.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal agents (MMAs) are AI systems that sustain interaction through reasoning, memory, tools, and feedback, most visibly as computer-use agents that operate software interfaces. Robotic systems (RSs), by contrast, couple sensing and actuation under physical dynamics such as torque limits, contact, and latency. Multimodal embodied agents (MMEAs) combine multimodal task reasoning with physical action and revise decisions based on feedback, raising the question of what changes when an agent moves from computer-use to robot-use.

## [Quant-Arena benchmarks LLM agents on end-to-end quantitative trading research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30113) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Quant-Arena introduces a benchmark and public leaderboard that evaluates whether LLM agents can complete the full quantitative research loop—forming a hypothesis, implementing an executable signal, and backtesting—using real A-share data, with feedback limited to 2022–2023 and final assessment on a sealed 2024 test year. Across nine frontier models, GPT-6 Astra scored highest, yet no model beat the equal-weight market benchmark in 2024. This is a meaningful methodological step for evaluating LLM agents in finance, because it tests end-to-end research capability and out-of-sample generalization rather than isolated formula writing or single trading decisions. It sets a realistic bar showing that even top models struggle to beat a simple equal-weight market portfolio under trading costs and constraints. The evaluator uses a cash-account model with trading constraints and costs, including market impact estimated from a simulated order book, and scores are dominated by realized excess return while also considering signal quality and feature diversity. The benchmark is open to submissions from any model or agent harness, but it is a niche benchmark focused on A-share data rather than a broad paradigm shift.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Quantitative research is an iterative loop in which a researcher proposes a market hypothesis, implements it as an executable signal, tests it on historical data, and decides what to keep. Existing benchmarks typically score formulas, code, or individual trading decisions, leaving open whether LLMs can complete the full loop and produce strategies that generalize beyond the research period. A-share refers to RMB-denominated shares traded on China's mainland exchanges, and out-of-sample testing on a sealed future year is a standard way to guard against overfitting.

## [Environment Duels benchmark tests LLMs as both authors and solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30116) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Environment Duels is a new dynamic agentic benchmark in which each of nine frontier LLMs writes interactive environments with a privileged hint, aiming for tasks that capable solvers fail without the hint and pass with it. The study finds that solving and authoring are distinct skills, and that pairwise duels reveal transitive rankings, convergent designs, and hint-lift patterns that predict reinforcement-learning gains. This benchmark moves LLM evaluation beyond static question answering toward a two-sided ecosystem view, where models are judged on how well they teach and challenge each other. It could influence how agentic capabilities are measured and how synthetic training data and environments are generated and selected. The benchmark uses pairwise head-to-head duels and includes a reinforcement-learning study with Qwen3.8-27B, showing that a learner's hint lift on an author's environments tracks how much training on those environments improves it on held-out reasoning and agentic benchmarks. Notably, smaller models learn more from others' play and write the hardest environments and most useful hints, while the strongest solvers are not the strongest authors.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Agentic benchmarks evaluate LLMs on multi-step, long-horizon interaction with environments rather than single-turn answers, often requiring tool use and outcome verification. Environment Duels builds on this paradigm by making the environments themselves model-generated, turning evaluation into a co-evolutionary loop where models both create and solve tasks.

## [PhysiCalWorld calibrates world models via in-context physics prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30125) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PhysiCalWorld introduces a method that calibrates action-conditioned world models to instance-specific physics by encoding an exploratory interaction clip from the test scene into a compact physics descriptor token, which is then used as in-context prompting. The model is trained end-to-end with only latent prediction loss, without physics labels or fine-tuning, and the descriptor token is extracted once at inference and reused across rollouts with negligible overhead. This addresses a known failure mode in world models where predictions drift toward an empirical prior in novel scenes because physics properties like mass distribution or friction are not observable from a single frame. The approach could improve rollout accuracy and planning success in robotics and model-based RL without requiring test-time optimization or physics supervision. The method is evaluated across four environments covering diverse physics variations, showing substantial improvements in rollout prediction accuracy and visual planning success rate. The learned descriptors form a physically structured latent space and generalize beyond the physics ranges seen during training, though the work is a single arXiv preprint without community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: World models are learned simulators that predict future observations conditioned on actions, enabling planning and control in robotics and reinforcement learning. Action-conditioned world models typically rely on current visual frames or short history windows, which may not reveal latent physics properties such as mass distribution, joint damping, or surface friction that determine transition dynamics. In-context prompting allows a model to adapt to new tasks or environments by conditioning on examples without updating weights.

## [DFAH Harness Measures Determinism and Faithfulness of Financial LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.15322) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the Determinism-Faithfulness Assurance Harness (DFAH), a framework and protocol for replaying tool-using financial agents and comparing their behavior at three levels: final decisions, tool-name sets, and ordered tool sequences. Using 2,957 recorded executions in synthetic compliance-triage and data-operations tasks, it finds that one data-operations configuration repeated its recorded decision in 84% of cases but its ordered tool-name sequence in only 26%. Financial institutions deploying LLM agents face regulatory demands for auditable decision trails, yet LLM nondeterminism undermines reproducibility. DFAH provides a practical protocol for designing replay experiments and locating execution differences, helping developers certify that agents can be replayed for compliance, risk management, and post-mortem analysis. The framework treats support from available evidence as a separate evaluation target from decision and tool-sequence comparison, and a case-level example shows repeated labels accompanied by different lookups and escalation calls. The results demonstrate that the comparison level changes the description of repeated behavior, and some compliance cases retained the same tool sequence while recorded decisions changed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Tool-using LLM agents are AI systems that call external tools such as databases or APIs to complete tasks, and in finance they are increasingly used for compliance triage and data operations. Replaying an agent means rerunning it under specified conditions and comparing its behavior to a preserved execution record, but LLMs can produce different outputs for identical inputs. DFAH organizes this comparison into separate measurements of determinism (whether decisions repeat) and evidence-conditioned faithfulness (whether decisions are traceably tied to retrieved evidence).

## [Theory explains why masked diffusion models resist the reversal curse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02133) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2602.02133v3) provides a theoretical analysis showing that masked diffusion models (MDMs) mitigate the reversal curse through parameter-level coupling between forward and reverse positional conditionals, enabled by shared Transformer parameters and relative positional encodings. The authors prove that in a one-layer MDM, forward masked training strengthens evidence reusable in reverse queries and induces correlated forward-reverse attention routes, with controlled experiments and large-scale LLaDA/Dream tests confirming these signatures. This work challenges the common explanation that MDMs' any-order masked training objective alone accounts for the mitigation, offering a mechanistic understanding that could guide the design of future language models less prone to the reversal curse. It is a high-value theoretical contribution for researchers in language modeling and diffusion models, potentially influencing how positional encodings and parameter sharing are leveraged to improve bidirectional generalization. The analysis focuses on a one-layer MDM and proves that forward masked training yields a positively aligned shared-storage gradient component that decreases the reverse loss to first order; the authors also show that relative positional encodings route attention through queries and keys without altering the value-side evidence being retrieved. The theoretical predictions are validated on controlled one-layer experiments and large-scale LLaDA/Dream models, demonstrating improved reverse prediction.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: The reversal curse refers to the failure of autoregressive language models (ARMs) to generalize from a learned fact like "A is B" to the reverse query "B is A". Masked diffusion models are a class of generative models for discrete data that generate text by reversing a progressive masking process, and they have been shown to suffer from this failure much less than ARMs. Relative positional encodings are a technique that encodes token positions by their relative distances rather than absolute indices, which can improve generalization.

## [2-Step Agent: Bayesian Decision Maker Learns from AI Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.21889) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the 2-Step Agent framework, which models how a rational Bayesian agent updates beliefs and makes causal decisions based on predictions from an ML model. For the multivariate linear-Gaussian setting, the authors derive a tractable solution for the Bayesian inference problem and show that a single misaligned prior can cause ML decision support to worsen outcomes even with a well-specified model and a perfectly rational agent. This work provides a theoretical foundation for understanding when and why ML-based decision support helps or harms human decisions, which is critical for high-stakes domains like healthcare and the judiciary. It highlights that even under ideal conditions, decision support can backfire, underscoring the need to account for users' prior beliefs. The framework captures how an ML prediction informs the agent about both the training data and the underlying causal mechanism, affecting causal effect estimation and downstream decisions. The tractable solution is specific to multivariate linear-Gaussian models, and the experiments identify conditions under which ML-DS is beneficial compared to unassisted decision making.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Bayesian inference is a method of statistical inference where Bayes' theorem is used to update the probability of a hypothesis as more evidence becomes available. Causal inference is the process of determining the independent effect of a phenomenon, which is essential for making informed decisions. Multivariate linear-Gaussian models are a common setting in statistics where variables follow a joint normal distribution and relationships are linear, allowing for tractable analytical solutions.

## [FactReview verifies paper claims via literature and code execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04074) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FactReview is a new framework that verifies claims in scientific papers by checking them against the literature and executing the paper's released code to test empirical claims. Across 300 papers, it ranked best on five of seven metrics, achieving 6.8% false support and 79.2% classification accuracy, and in a 36-paper study, reviewers using it detected 9.4 issues per paper versus 5.6 independently while spending 74.1% less time. This work addresses a key gap in AI-assisted peer review by grounding claim verification in evidence rather than relying on superficial assessment, potentially improving the reliability of feedback for researchers and autonomous research systems. It could influence future AI reviewer systems and help accelerate research iteration by catching overlooked or misjudged issues. The system extracts review-relevant claims, grounds them in related work, and executes released artifacts under a fixed repair budget when code is available; across 35 ML papers and 463 benchmark major claims, it covered 84% of claims. The code is publicly available on GitHub, and the approach combines claim extraction, literature positioning, and execution-based verification.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Peer review is the process by which scientific papers are evaluated by experts before publication, but it is time-consuming and reviewers under pressure may miss critical issues. AI reviewers have emerged to assist, but they often assess claims without adequately verifying the supporting evidence, leading to unreliable feedback. FactReview aims to fill this gap by automatically checking claims against literature and running code to test empirical results.

## [Anytime-valid statistical framework for adaptive AI audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07002) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.07002v2) introduces a hypothesis testing framework for adaptive audits of generative AI systems that provides anytime-valid statistical guarantees under flexible sampling and stopping rules. The framework uses Safe Anytime-Valid Inference (SAVI) and 'testing by betting' to create simultaneous e-processes for dueling null hypotheses, and proves that passing such an audit certifies global robustness when the auditor is sufficiently powerful. This work addresses a key bottleneck in AI evaluation: the difficulty of drawing statistically rigorous conclusions from highly adaptive, small-sample audits. It enables auditors to make valid inferences even when sampling and stopping decisions are made during data collection, which could significantly improve the reliability and efficiency of AI safety and quality assessments. The framework formalizes the auditor as conducting 'testing by betting' with simultaneous e-processes for two dueling null hypotheses: the model's null (no failure mode below a threshold) and the auditor's null (a sampling strategy exists to uncover a failure mode). Empirically, the procedures maintain anytime-valid Type-I error control, outperform pre-specified testing methods, and can reach statistically rigorous conclusions with as few as 20 observations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Generative AI systems often have failure modes that are costly and time-consuming to annotate, leading to adaptive testing where auditors decide which cases to annotate based on past results. However, such adaptive sampling and optional stopping violate classical statistical assumptions, making it hard to draw rigorous conclusions. Anytime-valid inference, using tools like e-values and e-processes, provides statistical guarantees that remain valid under optional stopping and continuation, addressing this challenge.

## [WindINR: Latent-State Implicit Neural Representation for Fast Local Wind Queries and Correction in Complex Terrain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09511) ⭐️ 7.0/10

WindINR is a latent-state implicit neural representation framework that enables continuous high-resolution local wind queries and correction from sparse observations in complex terrain. It separates reusable representation learning from sample-specific latent correction, using a privileged encoder during training and a deployable predictor at inference, with discrepancies defining a dataset-adaptive Gaussian prior over latent corrections. This approach provides an efficient interface between kilometer-scale background wind products, sparse local observations, and local wind queries, which is crucial for applications like helicopter and UAV operations in mountainous regions. It outperforms baselines including WindSeer and achieves a 14-fold speedup over full-network fine-tuning, making real-time local wind estimation more feasible. At inference, network weights remain fixed and only a low-dimensional latent state is updated through a regularized objective incorporating sparse observations and their uncertainty, preserving continuous queries at arbitrary spatial coordinates. The framework is trained on the large-scale FuXi-CFD dataset and evaluated using controlled observing system simulation experiments, simulated helicopter trajectories, and real UAV measurements over Tromsø.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Implicit neural representations (INRs), also known as neural fields, are neural networks that map continuous inputs like spatial coordinates to continuous outputs, enabling discretization-independent and differentiable representations. In wind field estimation, traditional methods often produce dense forecasts on fixed grids, but many applications require fast, location-specific wind estimates. WindINR leverages INRs to provide continuous queries and uses latent state correction to assimilate sparse observations without retraining the network.

## [Zero-Knowledge Verification of Frontier AI Training Proposed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05433) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.05433v3) proposes a zero-knowledge verification approach for frontier AI training that combines a pre-committed training specification, inter-node network observations, and live Merkle commitments to intermediate computation. The design includes a reference zkVM with native BF16/FP32 precompiles that checks committed floating-point computation bit-for-bit against declared semantics. This work challenges the prevailing view that zero-knowledge proofs are impractical at frontier scale, offering a concrete architecture that could enable governance-auditable training records. It has implications for AI governance regimes that rely on training compute thresholds, suggesting global training budgets may be more robust than per-run FLOP caps. The scheme uses genesis proofs for initialization and in-training step proofs for selected steps, with ex-ante attestations enforcing policy claims as running invariants; detection-probability formulas quantify sampling guarantees. The authors acknowledge that weight smuggling—reusing or merging weights trained elsewhere—remains a challenge, and they catalogue open research and engineering problems.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Zero-knowledge proofs allow one party to prove a computation was performed correctly without revealing the underlying data, and a zkVM is a virtual machine that generates such proofs for program execution. Frontier AI governance proposals often use cumulative training compute (measured in FLOP) as a threshold for identifying high-impact models, but verifying that compute without access to proprietary training runs is difficult. BF16 and FP32 are floating-point formats commonly used in AI training, and native precompiles in a zkVM would allow efficient proof of floating-point operations rather than emulating them in a finite field.

## [Synthetic Rationale Fine-Tuning Hurts Clinical Prediction, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10279) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A controlled study of 504 configurations finds that fine-tuning models with synthetic rationales (Rationale First SFT) consistently degrades five-year Alzheimer's disease and related dementias (ADRD) prediction compared to label-only fine-tuning, across model families and data scales. The degradation persists even with reasoning-oriented base models and is reproduced in a cardiovascular disease (CVD) validation. This counterintuitive finding challenges the common assumption that teaching models to explain their predictions improves clinical accuracy, which could affect how AI systems are developed for high-stakes healthcare settings. It suggests that rationale-based supervision may be harmful in certain prediction tasks, prompting a reevaluation of training strategies for clinical language models. The failure is not due to poor rationale quality: human experts confirmed the generated rationales are medically accurate and grounded in patient-specific evidence, and few-shot experiments show the same rationales improve performance when used as inference-time demonstrations. A label-ordering intervention—placing the prediction before the rationale—restores performance, indicating that conditioning predictions on generated rationales causes the degradation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is a common technique for adapting large language models to specific tasks by training them on input-output pairs. Synthetic rationales are machine-generated explanations that accompany correct labels, intended to teach models the reasoning behind predictions. Alzheimer's disease and related dementias (ADRD) prediction from longitudinal health records is an important clinical application where accurate five-year risk forecasting could enable early intervention.

## [TNODEV: First End-to-End Formal Verifier for Neural ODEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16567) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced TNODEV, the first formal verifier for neural ordinary differential equations (neural ODEs) that integrates a falsification checker, a continuous-time mixed-monotonicity reachability backend, a verification-refinement loop with three input-set splitting heuristics, and a parallel scheduler into a single pipeline. It supports safe-set inclusion verification for pure neural ODEs, neural ODEs in closed loop with neural network controllers, and general neural ODEs (GNODE), with safe sets specified as intervals or half-space intersections induced by target classification labels. Neural ODEs are increasingly used in safety-critical settings such as continuous-time controllers for cyber-physical systems and classifiers in automated decision pipelines, so the ability to formally verify their behavior is crucial. TNODEV's end-to-end pipeline with iterative refinement and parallel scheduling advances the precision and scalability of neural ODE verification, potentially enabling certification of learned models in safety-critical applications. TNODEV is evaluated on a range of benchmarks for safe-set inclusion and classification-robustness properties, including a direct reachability comparison against NNV 2.0 and CORA and a verification comparison against NNV 2.0 on MNIST general neural ODE classifiers. The tool's precision depends on the input-set splitting heuristics and the refinement loop, which iteratively tighten bounds to improve verdict accuracy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Neural ODEs are a class of continuous-depth models where the derivative of the hidden state is parameterized by a neural network, allowing the network to be seen as solving an ODE. Formal verification of neural ODEs aims to prove that for all inputs in a given set, the system's output remains within a safe region, which is challenging due to the continuous dynamics. Mixed-monotonicity reachability is a technique that over-approximates reachable sets by decomposing the system into increasing and decreasing parts, and input-set splitting heuristics divide the input domain to refine the verification.

## [New Rule Violation Score Measures Logical Compliance of Predictive Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20208) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce the Rule Violation Score (RVS), a complementary metric that quantifies how well predictive models respect logical and domain-specific constraints, independently of accuracy. RVS treats hard rules and soft rules differently and can be computed via automatically generated SQL queries for Horn rules. In high-stakes domains like healthcare, finance, and autonomous systems, logical consistency can be as critical as predictive accuracy, yet no standard metric captures this dimension. RVS reveals behavioral differences between models with similar accuracy, helping practitioners select safer and more trustworthy models. RVS can be evaluated on any dataset and any predictive model expressed over a relational vocabulary, and it can also assess the logical consistency of training datasets and help identify poorly defined rules. The authors evaluate RVS on three benchmarks covering knowledge graph link prediction and relational regression, including rule-based, embedding-based, and neuro-symbolic models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Machine learning models are typically evaluated using predictive performance metrics such as accuracy, ranking quality, or prediction error, which measure how closely predictions match ground truth but ignore whether outputs obey predefined logical or domain-specific constraints. Logical rules can be hard (strict constraints) or soft (statistical regularities), and ensuring compliance is especially important in high-stakes applications. RVS fills this gap by providing a separate score for logical compliance that complements traditional accuracy metrics.

## [DFAH-Bench Reveals Decision Agreement Masks Tool Path Instability in Financial Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20491) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DFAH-Bench, a new replay benchmark, shows that financial agents' repeated decisions agree at 94-95% while their underlying tool paths agree only 45-52% across 570 eligible replay episodes. A separate 48-execution diagnostic found that deleting the same tool calls from repeated records left agreement perfect, and banking probes showed repeated authorization decisions sometimes conflicted with constructed rule labels. This exposes a critical blind spot in agreement-based agent evaluation: high decision agreement can conceal changes in execution, systematic omissions, and unsupported decisions. The findings suggest that reliability evaluation must connect what the agent did, the evidence and rules governing its action, and whether it completed the task, affecting how financial AI agents are validated and deployed. The τ-Knowledge banking study scheduled 1,080 episodes with 1,033 known outcomes; structural checks alone completed more primary tasks than either model-based gate-and-recovery bundle, and the typed-choice bundle had lower mean episode cost but fewer successes than the generative bundle. Missing outcomes prevented planned confirmatory tests, so banking comparisons are descriptive, and input limits and recovery behavior materially shape results.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: DFAH-Bench is a replay benchmark that measures observable behavioral instability in financial agent decision-making across three channels: tool-call trajectories, evidence contacts, and decision concentration, none of which require access to hidden reasoning text. It builds on the τ-Bench family, including τ-Knowledge and τ³-Banking, which evaluate agents on coordinating external knowledge with tool outputs to produce verifiable, policy-compliant state changes. Replay-based evaluation is increasingly used to compare agent trajectories and tool-call evidence against baselines in CI gates.

## [DeepLook: Uncertainty-Guided Branching Cuts Reasoning Tokens by 87%](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22602) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DeepLook introduces a training-free decoding framework that monitors segment-level confidence, branches only at uncertainty bottlenecks, and ranks candidate continuations by Average Lookahead Confidence (ALC) before voting over a pruned subset. On four competition math benchmarks across four open models, it matches or exceeds DeepConf-low accuracy in 12 of 16 settings while generating 87.3% fewer tokens. This work shows that a model's own confidence can decide where extra decoding compute is worth spending, offering a path to more efficient test-time scaling for LLM reasoning. It could significantly reduce inference costs for reasoning-heavy applications without sacrificing accuracy on math tasks. On knowledge-intensive GPQA-Diamond, the accuracy advantage disappears even though token savings remain, because coherent hallucinations also receive high ALC; internal confidence localizes instability, not falsehood. The method is training-free and code is available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 23, 04:00

**Background**: Test-time scaling improves LLM reasoning by spending more compute during inference, but typically does so uniformly along a reasoning trace, even in stable stretches. DeepConf is a related method that uses token-level confidence to filter low-quality reasoning traces. DeepLook builds on these ideas by using confidence to guide branching and ranking, aiming to allocate compute more efficiently.

## [CTBench: New Benchmark Exposes AI Agent Gaps in Telecom Troubleshooting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12002) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CTBench, a public benchmark built by domain experts to evaluate AI agents on root cause analysis (RCA) and path restoration in realistic, partially observable telecom network environments. The evaluation found that state-of-the-art agents perform well at identifying path endpoints but severely underperform in RCA, and often fail to collect the diagnostic evidence required in operational practice even when their final answers are correct. Telecom operators are increasingly exploring autonomous agents to reduce troubleshooting time, so a benchmark that measures diagnostic reasoning rather than just final answers gives a more realistic picture of deployment readiness. The finding that more compute does not improve RCA suggests current agent architectures have structural gaps that scaling alone will not fix. CTBench includes rich metadata and golden evidence trajectories, and its metrics score both final accuracy and the diagnostic reasoning path. The benchmark explicitly models partial observability and heterogeneity, two real-world telecom characteristics that earlier evaluations failed to capture.
